#3132. This Janus Henderson fund manager owns CoreWeave debt — here's why he's sticking with it

CoreWeave can overcome the market's skepticism about its debt.

That isn't the consensus view — some Coreweave debt remains deeply distressed with yields above 15%. But Coreweave should power through this tough stretch, according to John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson.

"It's a little bit of overreacting," Lloyd tells CNBC, referring to the sell-off in Coreweave bonds, which have tanked along with its equity.

Lloyd owns Coreweave 9.75% bonds due in 2031 in the firm's Multi-Sector Income Fund. The yield to maturity on the debt is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since its issuance in April. Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.

"Bond prices are lower, spreads are wider, yields are wider, and I don't see a huge fundamental change in the ecosystem right now that would warrant that, other than their higher cost of financing, which I think they can get creative with," Lloyd added.

CoreWeave has taken on a massive amount of debt to fund its data center buildout. The company has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, including about $35 billion in notes, bonds and loans.

The market is getting nervous about how CoreWeave will be able to keep financing its data centers, and at what cost. Its stock has tumbled 20% over the past week, while the spread on its five-year credit default swaps is nearly 1,081 basis points — lower than earlier in the session but up 13% week over week. Spreads of 1,000 basis points and above are generally a sign of credit distress.

CoreWeave shares moved roughly 2% higher on Tuesday, after tumbling over the past several days and hitting a 52-week low earlier in the session. Its bond yields and spreads remain highly elevated.

Lloyd believes the selloff is a bump in the road for the neocloud company.

The market has been spooked by the jump in Treasury rates, which pushes up funding costs for companies, he explained. The focus largely turned to companies like CoreWeave that have refinance risk when rates move higher, he said.

CoreWeave leverage is high with a debt-to-equity ratio of 702%, up from 290% a year ago. Moreover, CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capital expenditures this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

Revenue and Ebitda will ramp up next year, hitting $26.5 billion and $16.7 billion respectively, analysts estimate. But capex will hit $47.9 billion, indicating the company may need to finance its data center buildout with more equity issuance and/or debt.

Lloyd isn't necessarily worried about CoreWeave's ability to finance its capital expenditures, despite the fact that its capex outweighs its free-cash flow.

"Next year … they're going to be generating operating free-cash flow that'll support some of that capex need," he said.

The company can also turn to other methods of funding, like more GPU financing, which will allow it to borrow against its graphics processing units or rental contracts, he noted. In addition, CoreWeave could ask its biggest customers — including Microsoft, Meta Platforms and OpenAI — to increase their prepayments, he added.

"They can probably get creative enough where they don't even need to come to the high-yield market," Lloyd said.

The company also doesn't have any near-term maturities, which means it isn't facing a refinancing risk anytime soon, he added.

Meanwhile the supply/demand dynamic should remain tight across the AI compute landscape. Overall adoption rates within the ecosystem are running above consensus demand curves, he said. Add to that a bottleneck in supply, with chips and memory power sold out, and it's a nice set up for CoreWeave.

"All data point to the business model functioning very, very well," Lloyd said. "CoreWeave is one of the best-in-class at getting data centers up and actually running them," he added, noting that CoreWeave's financials have been improving throughout the year.

Another turning point could come if Anthropic goes forth with an IPO, expected in November, followed by OpenAI. Successful IPOs for the frontier labs could soothe market nerves and help support the neocloud operators.

"You just need more of those data points where people become more comfortable with the AI demand side," Lloyd said.

CoreWeave still needs to convince markets it can handle higher market rates and get through the financing bottlenecks.

Lloyd sees the company making it, albeit with some bumps along the way. The bonds should recover, he says, "just not in that straight line."

#3131. Palantir Stock Just Fell 8 Days in a Row. History Says What Followed Its Only 2 Longer Losing Streaks.

Did the AI software company's two longer slides say anything about what happened next?

Palantir Technologies (PLTR +3.48%) stock closed lower for eight straight trading sessions through Thursday, Nov. 5. Over that span, shares dropped from $198.84 to $154.99, a fall of around 22%.

That put the AI software maker's stock about 26% under its record close on Oct. 9.

By my count of daily closes since Palantir started trading in September 2020, only two losing streaks have lasted longer. One was a 10-day slide ending May 10, 2021. The other was a nine-day slide ending March 1, 2023.

Each time, shares were up six months later. But a year out, the two cases ended up far apart. And two cases is a tiny sample, so I wouldn't rely on the streak to say much about what happens next.

Interestingly, both earlier slides were close in size to this one. The 2021 streak took the stock from $24.11 to $18.47, a fall of around 23%. The 2023 streak took shares from $10.11 to $7.69, a decline of around 24%.

(I measured what followed from each streak's final close, treating 21 trading days as a month.)

After the 2021 slide, Palantir stock gained around 31% in the next month and was up about 24% three months out. Six months later, it was about 41% above that final close. But a year after the slide ended, shares were down about 60% to $7.46.

The 2023 case went the other way over the full year. Shares were up only around 6% a month later. In the next three months, though, they rose by about 91%, and they were up 112% after six months.

Even more, one year out, the stock had more than tripled, gaining around 224% to $24.93.

The two seven-day streaks, which ended in September 2022 and June 2026, don't settle much either. After the 2022 slide, shares climbed about 16% in a month, dipped about 1% over three months, and were up about 12% after six months. A year out, the stock had more than doubled.

The June 2026 slide is too recent for a six-month read. But once it ended at $107.27, shares gained around 23% in the next month and about 80% in three.

So, after all four of Palantir's longest losing streaks before this one, the stock was up a month later. But with 12-month returns ranging from a 60% loss to a 224% gain, the streak arguably told investors little about the year ahead.

One difference between 2021 and 2023 sticks out to me, and it's the starting price. At $18.47, Palantir's stock traded at around 30 times its 2020 sales. At $7.69 in 2023, it cost under 9 times its 2022 sales. And Palantir lost money in both those years, though it had posted its first quarterly profit right before the 2023 slide started.

Today, Palantir's market value is more than 50 times its sales for the last four quarters, a higher sales multiple than in either case.

Sure, the business is very different now. Palantir's third-quarter revenue climbed 96% year over year to around $2.3 billion. This kept a streak of faster growth going, after rates of 70%, 85%, and 93% in the three quarters before that. And Palantir now turns a big part of its sales into profit, with a 47% operating margin last quarter.

Not every number is speeding up, though. The total contract value of the deals Palantir closed in the third quarter rose just 6% from a year before. The growth rate had already slowed sharply in the previous three quarters, dropping from 138% to 61% and then 49%. Deal timing can make the number lumpy. But if it keeps slowing, revenue growth might eventually follow.

Management's fourth-quarter revenue guidance of about $2.65 billion already implies growth of about 88%, down from the third quarter's 96%.

Palantir made $1.43 a share in its past four quarters. So at roughly $160 as I write, the stock trades at about 112 times earnings. Earlier this week, I put my buy price at 120 times those earnings, or about $172 a share.

In the end, the length of this streak doesn't tell me much about where Palantir stock heads next. What matters more to me is that shares now trade below my buy price.

Yes, a growth stock that just dropped 22% over eight sessions could easily keep swinging. But I think shares are worth a look at this level, and I'd build a position slowly instead of all at once.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Palantir Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3129. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 6: A Little Lower

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates dropped today, but inflation data could shake things up next week.

With the midterm elections behind us, mortgage rates are continuing their gradual slide this month. One factor is a sell-off in tech stocks, which can send investors toward the relative safety of bonds and help push mortgage rates lower.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 6.96% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is five basis points lower than yesterday and 11 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Meanwhile, a stronger-than-expected jobs report could slow that slide by reinforcing expectations for another Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

And a reminder: While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

The recent slide in mortgage rates might reverse course if next week’s inflation data comes in hot.

This week, the Nerds are watching the October Consumer Price Index, which comes out on Tuesday, Nov. 10. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at the other data set, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Right now, the labor market is looking pretty darn healthy: The October jobs report, released today from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, showed U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, far more than economists expected, and unemployment edged down to 4.1%.

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, but rate hikes are only on the table when the labor market is strong enough to handle it. Right now, it’s looking fairly likely that the Fed will approve a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Still, a jump in mortgage rates isn’t a sure thing. October’s inflation data will capture the effect of the Fed’s last rate hike, which came in the middle of September. If that worked as expected and inflation shows signs of slowing, mortgage rates will be less affected.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.46% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3126. Wall Street Is Ignoring Bristol Myers Squibb's Cash Machine -- and That's an Opportunity for Income Investors

Bristol Myers Squibb has a 4% yield and a 55% payout ratio, which should greatly interest dividend lovers.

Bristol Myers Squibb (BMY -1.57%) traces its history back to the mid-1800s. It is a proven survivor in the highly competitive and innovation-driven pharmaceutical sector. That can't save the company from the normal dynamics of the drug industry, as it works through a period of elevated patent expirations. Investor concerns about patent expirations, however, have turned the company into a dividend machine, yielding 4%. The average pharma stock yields just 1.4%.

Here's why Bristol Myers Squibb could be a buying opportunity for long-term income investors. And it all boils down to the success of the company's "growth portfolio," which is increasingly powering its cash flow.

The pharmaceutical sector follows a normal cycle that every company goes through. To simplify it, a company invests heavily in research and development to identify and secure approval for new drugs; the drugs have strong sales during a period of patent exclusivity; and then sales fall off when the patent expires and generic competition enters the market.

Investors inevitably get worried when patent expirations come up, because it means revenues are going to come under pressure. Right now, Bristol Myers Squibb has some big patent expirations on the horizon, and the stock's price has been relatively weak. However, dividend investors need to put this situation into perspective. The healthcare giant still has a well-covered dividend, highlighted by a 55% trailing 12-month payout ratio in the third quarter.

And revenues from what the company calls its "legacy portfolio" aren't exactly falling off a cliff just yet. In the third quarter, sales from this collection of drugs rose 1%. These drugs represent just over 40% of revenues. The other 60% is the cash flow machine you want to watch.

In the third quarter, the drugs Bristol Myers Squibb puts in its "growth portfolio" generated 12% revenue growth. The list of drugs here includes Opdivo Qvantig, Breyanzi, Reblozyl, Camzyos, and Opdualag. Don't worry too much about the names; just note that it is a fairly long list. This isn't a one-trick pony business. Bristol Myers Squibb is a respected, diversified industry giant with a long and successful history in drug development. And it is already seeing material success on that front.

To be fair, investors aren't exactly ignoring this fact. As the chart below highlights, the stock is up from its lows, and the dividend yield isn't as attractive as it was when it was roughly 6%. Still, the yield is well above the market average and that of the average pharma stock, so there's a very good reason for dividend investors to jump on this cash-flow machine.

The 40% of revenues that fall into the "legacy portfolio" is a problem. And investors need to monitor what's going on with that portfolio. However, it is entirely normal for drugs to become less profitable as generic competition enters the market. So Bristol Myers Squibb is just working through a transition period that it has worked through many times before. If history is any guide, it will survive this period in relative stride.

All of that said, the real issue for Bristol Myers Squibb is the drug Eliquis, which had sales of nearly $4.7 billion in the third quarter. It accounts for most of the company's $5.4 billion of "legacy portfolio" revenues. It will be a big hit when this drug loses patent protection, and there's a lot riding on finding a replacement.

But the "growth portfolio", which generates revenues of $7.6 billion, includes a dozen drugs, and revenue growth across the group has been strong. If you recognize the cash flow machine that's building in this portfolio, you might find Bristol Myers Squibb and its above-average yield an attractive addition to your passive income portfolio.

Reuben Gregg Brewer has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Bristol Myers Squibb. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3118. Higher Yield vs. Lower Cost: The Critical Differences Between HDV and VIG

VIG emphasizes dividend growers with lower costs, while HDV targets concentrated high-yielding stocks. One delivered 34.6% returns over the past year; the other offers greater stability.

Vanguard Dividend Appreciation ETF (VIG -0.38%) focuses on companies with a history of increasing their payouts, while iShares Core High Dividend ETF (HDV -0.69%) targets a concentrated group of high-yielding domestic equities.

Dividend-focused investors often choose between maximizing current income or betting on long-term capital growth through dividend appreciation. VIG tracks companies that have consistently increased dividends, while HDV selects stocks offering notably high yields today. This comparison examines how their different strategies affect cost, risk, and portfolio composition.

Beta measures price volatility relative to the S&P 500; beta is calculated from monthly returns over the available fund history (up to five years). The 1-yr return represents total return over the trailing 12 months. Dividend yield is the trailing-12-month distribution yield.

The Vanguard fund is the more affordable choice, with a 0.04% expense ratio, which is exactly half the iShares fund's cost. However, investors seeking higher current income may find the iShares fund more appealing, as it offers a trailing yield that is double its peer's distribution.

Vanguard Dividend Appreciation ETF holds 333 positions and emphasizes growth-oriented sectors. Its largest holdings include Microsoft (MSFT -0.99%) at 4.90%, Apple (AAPL -0.52%) at 4.89%, and Broadcom (AVGO +0.74%) at 4.30%. The portfolio leans heavily toward Technology at 27%, Financial Services at 21%, and Healthcare at 19%. The fund was launched in 2006. Vanguard Dividend Appreciation ETF has paid $3.65 per share over the trailing 12 months, which, on its recent ~$248.24 share price, works out to a 1.5% yield.

iShares Core High Dividend ETF maintains a tighter portfolio of 74 holdings, focusing on stability and current income. Its largest positions include Exxonmobil (XOM +0.34%) at 9.61%, Chevron (CVX -0.15%) at 7.26%, and Abbvie (ABBV +1.06%) at 6.80%. This concentrated mix is heavy on Consumer Staples (27%) and Energy (25%), with Healthcare contributing 13%. The fund was launched in 2011. iShares Core High Dividend ETF has paid $0.96 per share over the trailing 12 months, which, on its recent ~$30.99 share price, works out to a 3.1% yield.

For more guidance on ETF investing, check out the full guide at this link.

When comparing the Vanguard Dividend Appreciation ETF (VIG) and the iShares Core High Dividend ETF (HDV), investors should consider several key factors. Let's see what they tell us about each fund.

First, we should examine each fund's strategy. VIG targets U.S. stocks with at least 10 years of growing annual dividend payments. HDV, on the other hand, targets top U.S. dividend-paying stocks based on yield. VIG also holds many more stocks, with around 330 names, while HDV holds about 75.

Two other important factors are historical performance and income potential. Since 2021, VIG has generated a total return of 60%, equating to a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 9.8%. HDV, meanwhile, has delivered an 83% total return, with a 12.9% CAGR. As for direct income potential, HDV delivers more. Its dividend yield of 3.1% is more than twice that of VIG's 1.5.%.

One final factor to weigh is cost. Here, both funds are quite affordable. VIG has the lower annual expense ratio, though, at 0.04%, while HDV's 0.08% is slightly higher.In summary, VIG and HDV both hold dividend stocks, yet they differ in many key respects. HDV delivers higher absolute income and has also generated superior total returns in recent years. As such, many income-oriented investors will favor HDV. That said, VIG could still appeal to extremely cost-conscious investors, given its lower expense ratio.

Jake Lerch has positions in ExxonMobil. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends AbbVie, Apple, Broadcom, Chevron, Microsoft, and Vanguard Dividend Appreciation ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3113. This Warren Buffett Dividend Stock Is the One I'd Buy With $1,000 in November

Warren Buffett's longtime investment offers a straightforward way to benefit from consumer spending.

A $1,000 investment doesn't need to prove its worth immediately. Indeed, the goal for investors should be to buy a piece of a business that can grow its earnings -- and distributions -- for years, and to hold onto that stake long term. American Express (AXP -1.23%) is one such company. Berkshire Hathaway's (BRKA -0.01%) (BRKB -0.25%) longtime association with American Express might put it on some investors' watch lists, but its economics might be what earns it a place in your portfolio.

First, we need to understand American Express' business model. In a nutshell, the company provides a suite of payment-based services. It issues credit cards and charge cards (which are not the same thing), collects fees and interest from the consumers who use them, collects transaction fees from merchants that process payments on its cards, and offers lending services to borrowers. Overall, its business is solid. Revenue grew 9% year over year in its most recent quarter. Billed business increased by 8%.

American Express attracts an affluent customer base that is generally better able to weather economic downturns. Net card fees (the annual fees paid by cardholders regardless of spending volume) provide the company with a subscription-like recurring revenue stream that can help cushion the company from the impact of temporary consumer-spending slowdowns.

That said, like all lenders, its success depends in part on its ability to keep credit losses down. That means that the risk of a severe economic downturn that could lead to a spike in defaults is always one worth considering.

One reason that Berkshire Hathaway has kept American Express such a large part of its stock portfolio for so long is that the company continues to drive shareholder value. In part, it looks like this:

As you can see, its earnings per share have increased roughly threefold since 2016. Granted, there were a few dips along the way, but for the most part, earnings grew steadily. Similarly, the company's annual dividend payments have also nearly tripled.

However, even with its dividend growing nearly threefold in just 10 years, the dividend yield remains a modest 1.1% at the current share price. That means two things. First, management is prudent. They are ensuring that the company can withstand any credit losses that it may have to write off during an economic downturn. Second, by maintaining a moderate rate of dividend increases, management has left itself ample room to raise the dividend further and drive additional shareholder value if economic conditions improve.

The overall case for owning American Express stock is pretty straightforward: It's an iconic brand with a loyal, affluent customer base. Moreover, steady earnings and dividend growth make it a strong building block for a long-term portfolio. Finally, a $1,000 initial investment is a nice middle ground between making a stock a core investment and a symbolic one.

Now we must turn to valuation. Currently, American Express shares trade at a price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple of 18.4. That's just below its 10-year average P/E of 19.5. What's more, it's well below the S&P 500 average multiple of around 25.9. All in all, the stock is affordable, but it's not the cheapest equity you'll find on Wall Street.

As for risks, they are what you might expect for a financial stock. A recession is the main one. Economic downturns often result in slower consumer spending and a rise in unpaid bills -- both of which would dent American Express' bottom line.

In conclusion, American Express is my pick for a Buffett dividend stock to recommend investors buy this month. Its consistent revenue and earnings growth, coupled with rising dividend payments and an affordable valuation, mean it ticks all the core boxes for a Berkshire stock: It's an ideal investment to buy when you're ready to let compound growth and steady dividend reinvestment work over long-term horizons rather than chasing quick gains.

#3110. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3105. Nutex Health (NUTX) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Revenue slipped, but net income surged as compensation costs fell sharply.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Nutex Health Inc. (NUTX -6.75%) reported a decline in total revenue for the first nine months of 2026 compared to the prior year, attributing the change to adjustments in historical collection data and revenue recognition patterns from 2025. Management emphasized a 159% increase in net income for the period, which reflected lower stock-based compensation expenses and a non-cash reduction in contract services. The company reported continued growth in hospital volumes and highlighted a construction pipeline of new facilities scheduled for completion through 2028. Strategic objectives include establishing new provider networks to improve patient care coordination and utilizing federal dispute resolution frameworks to secure fair market-based reimbursement rates.

Thomas Vo: Thank you, Vivian, and good morning, everyone. I'm pleased to join you today to review Nutex Health's third quarter 2026 results. It was another strong quarter with solid financial results, steady collections, important reimbursement developments and continued growth in hospital volumes. I'll start with our financial and operational performance in the first nine months. For the first three quarters of 2026, total revenue reached $644.9 million, an 11% decrease from $723.6 million for the same period in 2025. Adjusted EBITDA decreased 13% from $243 million to $211.4 million for the first 3 quarters of 2026.

Net income attributable to Nutex increased to $152.9 million for the first 3 quarters of 2026, a 159% increase from $59 million for the same period in 2025. On the volume side, for the first 3 quarters of 2026, our hospitals recorded 154,300 total patient visits, up 10.2% from 140,100 during the same period in 2025. Same hospital growth was 6.3% in the first 9 months of 2026. On the balance sheet, net long-term debt went from $29.2 million at December 31, 2025, to $34.2 million at the end of Q3 2026, still very low relative to our revenue and our expansion pace.

Cash on hand grew to $249.9 million as of September 30, 2026, up from $185.6 million at year-end 2025. Net cash from operating activities was $188.1 million for the first 3 quarters of 2026 compared to $177.7 million in 2025, a 6% increase. Several factors supported our strong performance over the first nine months. Patient volume and acuity continue to rise, supported by renewed internal initiatives and investments. In particular, same hospital growth is significant for us as it means our growth strategy isn't wholly reliant on the opening of new hospitals. Earn-out expenses declined as most legacy facilities under development as of 2022 completed vesting, boosting net income.

Arbitration-related costs decreased following the renegotiation of the HALO MD contract in the second quarter of 2026. Collections remained strong through the third quarter of 2026. The investors may have noticed a decline in both revenue and adjusted EBITDA year-over-year through the first three quarters of 2026. The declines are not necessarily due to weaker collections in 2026, but rather due to an uneven revenue recognition for the first 9 months in 2025, primarily reflected by two factors. First, additional collection data available in 2025 supported higher collection estimates for 2024 services that was recognized in 2025. And second, ineligible charts were included from mid-2024 through the first three quarters of 2025 and adjusted in the fourth quarter of 2025.

We are also starting to see slight differences between collections for hospital versus collections from the physician entity. Physician fee collections are slightly down compared to hospital collection in the third quarter. This may account for some of the decreases in revenue in addition to the factors that I mentioned above. And as a reminder, physician revenue are consolidated in the revenue but eliminated down at the net income level. We are continuing to follow this trend closely. In addition, we are seeing more stabilization of revenue this year compared to this time last year. Lastly, as more internal hospital development commences, the long-term debt would increase accordingly in future quarters. Jon will discuss these details in his report.

Turning to reimbursement. The quarter included several developments that positively reinforce our long-term view of the No Surprises Act, NSA and the independent dispute resolution IDR process. First, as discussed on our last earnings call, federal courts in California, Florida, Pennsylvania, Texas, Connecticut and Georgia issued decisions during the first half of 2026, reinforcing the finality of the IDR award and limiting insurers' ability to challenge arbitration outcomes in courts. In the third quarter, the District Court in Virginia reached a similar conclusion in a case involving Blue Cross Blue Shield of Virginia, bringing the number of states with provider favorable ruling on key IDR issues to at least 7.

We believe these rulings support our view that the laws payment and arbitration requirements should be enforced as written. Second, recent antitrust and consumer protection against insurers by the campus and Michigan attorney generals highlight intensifying regulatory challenges to the activities of insurers, focusing on planned administrative practices, in particular, fiduciary management, hidden fee structures and the lack of transparency of out-of-network pricing mechanism. Among other things, the Kansas lawsuit alleges that insurers inflate the revenue by making below-market initial out-of-network offer in the IDR process, creating an artificial cost savings to maximize the percentage-based administrative fee payable to the insurer, which is tied directly to the suppress payment benchmarks. Third, as disclosed in our August press release, the U.S.

Court of Appeal for the Fifth Circuit issued an unbank decision addressing calculation of the qualifying payment amount or QPA, under the NSA. The court ruled in favor of the Texas Medical Association and other plaintiffs on two of the three issues presented in validating the use of those rates in federal QPA calculations. The QPA is an important benchmark in the IDR process. We have consistently maintained that the use of those rates and other flawed methodologies can distort QPA calculations, contributing to substantial provider underpayments and result in an increase in the volume of IDR disputes. We view the court's decision as a meaningful progress toward a more transparent and balanced reimbursement framework.

Over time, more accurate upfront payments without the use of those rates should reduce the rate for costly and time-consuming arbitration. Fourth, earlier this month, CMS released the results of two latest formal audits, including CareSource of Indiana and the School Employees Retirement System of Ohio. In the CareSource audit, CMS identified deficiencies involving required NSA disclosures, timely delivery of QPA-related information and calculation of the QPA under application regulations. The School Employees Retirement System audit similarly identified deficiencies involving the lack of appropriate QPA disclosures and IDR-related notification requirements. These findings provide further evidence of inconsistent insurer compliance and payment practices, including continued noncompliance with federal guidelines.

We believe stronger oversight and enforcement can help providers receive more timely and accurate reimbursement. Fifth, we are closely monitoring the legislative developments in Washington, D.C., including 2 recent proposals that take materially different approaches to the future of the IDR framework. The Murphy bill will preserve independent arbitration while strengthening enforcement mechanisms and adding penalties for payers that do not timely satisfy IDR awards. We strongly support the Murphy bill and are advocating for its passage. The Pallone bill by contrast, would eliminate the IDR process beginning in 2028 and generally make the payer calculate the QPA the final reimbursement amount for on-network services with limited review of the methodology used to calculate those rates.

We firmly oppose the Pallone proposal. We believe the independent review through the current IDR process is essential when ensuring that reimbursement rates fail to reflect the fair market values as they currently do most of the time. We also expect strong opposition from other provider organizations should the legislation advance. We are also aware of other nonemergency specialties, physician specialties, including anesthesiology, surgery, radiology and medical transportation companies that also use the IDR to seek fair reimbursement. though we are unfamiliar with their outcomes or eligibility. While the Pallone proposal's intended target is the overall IDR process, it is unclear to us which specialty he is targeting.

We suspect that the Pallone bill may aim to limit other nonemergency providers access to the IDR. Federal and state Emtala requirements obligate our hospitals to evaluate every single patient that enters our emergency rooms. We expect similarly insurers to meet their federal obligations under the Prudent Layperson Act by paying fair and reasonable reimbursement to Amtala participating hospitals and emergency medicine providers. We also have no control over all the providers that provide nonemergency services. Nutex's goal is very simple. We seek fair market-based reimbursement for medically necessary care in our communities with comparable payments for comparable services in comparable settings.

Federal laws enacted to achieve this result should be enforced and median in rates calculated accurately without those rates distortions. Turning to growth. We remain confident in our hospital development pipeline and the opportunities ahead. For the remainder of 2026, our active development pipeline includes projects in San Antonio, Texas and Jacksonville, Florida, both of which are expected to open in the fourth quarter. Looking ahead, we have 4 additional projects under active construction with expected completion in late 2027 and early 2028. These facilities represent the next phase of our long-term expansion strategy. Operationally, our priorities remain increasing patient volumes, expanding service lines and appropriately growing observation and inpatient care across the network.

We continue to invest in technology, diagnostics, artificial intelligence and differentiated patient service to enhance clinical quality, improve efficiency and scale the Nutex model. As new technologies become available, our hospitals will be able to offer a broader range of care than ever before. Patient satisfaction remains a core strength. As highlighted in recent press release, our hospitals continue to earn recognition for quality and service, reinforcing our position as a trusted provider for physicians, healthcare professionals and their families in the communities we serve. Wes will provide additional report in his report. Bottom line is that we continue to operate a profitable business with strong cash flow and robust internal and external growth.

The healthcare industry is undergoing significant transformation. Health care expenditures continue to place substantial pressure on employers, government programs, insurers and patients. At the same time, the population is aging, chronic conditions are becoming increasingly prevalent and the demand for accessible high-quality medical services continues to grow. The traditional fee-for-service model, which generally rewards the volume of services delivered is increasingly being supplemented by value-based care arrangements that emphasize quality, efficiency and patient outcomes.

We believe this transition represents a substantial long-term opportunity for organizations that can successfully manage patient populations, coordinate care and demonstrate measurable improvements in healthcare delivery. We believe our Population Health Management division is strategically positioned to participate in this transformation. Our approach is centered on the integration of physicians, facilities, technology and data analytics. By working collaboratively with providers and other healthcare partners, we seek to improve the coordination of care across the patient journey. Our core strategy revolves around physician networks. Our IPAs are comprised of networks of contracted and credentialed primary care physicians and specialists located around our facilities. Building strong partnerships with local doctors is critical.

By forming these IPAs, we are building awareness of our hospitals among the local community doctors and their patients. We believe that over time, these relationships will not only increase the volume of IPA and non-IPA patients to our hospitals, but also create a web of care that's seamless for patients. Our vision is that our hospitals and IPAs will work hand-in-hand to amplify our reach and effectiveness. We are fostering collaboration, sharing best practices and ensuring every provider is aligned with our patient-first culture. Going forward, our goal is to build one to two new IPAs around our hospitals every year. With that, I'll turn it over to Wes Bamburg, our Chief Operating Officer.

Wesley Bamburg: Thank you, Warren, and good morning, everyone. As Tom and Jon discussed, patient demand remained strong during the third quarter. I'll focus my comments this morning on how our hospitals are supporting that growth through clinical execution, business development and workforce stability. We saw that demand clearly in our patient volumes. Hospital visits increased 18.1% compared to the prior year, while same-hospital visits increased 12.4%. This growth reflects strong demand across our markets and continued execution at both newer and more established facilities. As our facilities mature, we continue to expand the range of care they can provide. We are investing in technology and clinical capabilities from advanced diagnostic imaging and specialty services that improve how we deliver care.

That includes the thoughtful use of AI and automation to support our clinical teams, streamline workflows and help physicians spend more time with patients. Together, these investments broaden the services available in each community, allowing us to serve more patients locally, strengthening continuity of care and appropriately grow observation and inpatient services across the network. The physician relationships that Warren just discussed are also important to this strategy. Stronger alignment with local primary care physicians and specialists can improve care coordination, increase awareness of our hospitals capabilities and help patients receive appropriate care closer to home. We are also leveraging business development more systematically across the organization. Each of our facilities has local business development leadership focused on its markets.

We're offering support through a corporate structure that coordinates strategy and shares best practices. So through community events, outreach, sponsorships, physician engagement and other local initiatives, we are strengthening community relationships and increasing awareness of the services our hospitals provide. On to workforce. Workforce stability remains another important component of our performance. Employee turnover was 9.6% through the first nine months of 2026. Maintaining an experienced workforce supports clinical continuity, operational consistency and our ability to respond as patient volumes increase. This has a direct impact on our patient experience, which remains a key priority for us.

Our hospitals maintained an average Google rating of 4.76 stars during the period, reflecting the daily work of our physicians, nurses and hospital teams to deliver high-quality patient-centered care every day. From an operational perspective, our priorities remain straightforward: align staffing with demand, improve productivity and resource utilization, standardize effective practices that drive volume growth and prepare our infrastructure to support future hospital openings. Overall, the third quarter demonstrated that our existing hospitals can support meaningful volume growth while maintaining workforce stability, patient experience and operating discipline. We remain focused on translating that momentum into consistent execution and sustainable growth across the enterprise. Thank you, everyone, and I'll turn the call back over to Vivian.

#3100. SpaceX's December Lockup Is Nearly the Biggest Share Unlock in IPO History. History Says This Is What Happens Next.

SpaceX conducted its IPO with a surprisingly low public float.

When Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +8.62%) conducted its initial public offering this summer, it bucked several long-standing trends. One of those trends was to go public by selling at least 10% of shares to the public. Many big tech companies, for example, initially offered 15% to 20% of their shares to the public during their IPOs. SpaceX, however, went public with just 5% of its total outstanding shares available for trade.

There's nothing inherently wrong about offering up only a tiny fraction of a company's outstanding shares for public purchase. But it does create challenges.

The S&P 500 index, for example, typically requires a public float of at least 10%. SpaceX's application to fast-track its inclusion into the index was denied. Low public float can also create additional volatility, as surges in buy or sell volumes can more easily overwhelm available supply. While several other factors were at play, the low public float likely contributed to SpaceX's early stock price volatility.

There is precedent for such a low initial public float. Saudi Aramco raised $25.6 billion with an initial public float of just 1.5%. SpaceX, however, raised a much larger $85.7 billion without upping its public float above 5%.

Very quickly after its IPO, however, SpaceX's public float began to rise. An additional 20% of shares became available when second-quarter earnings were released in August. October's third-quarter results freed up another 28%, with several other unlocking events throughout the adjacent months. In December, another 17% will become available for trade. That essentially frees up all of SpaceX's locked shares, minus the massive chunk of shares CEO Elon Musk will see unlocked next summer -- shares that are unlikely to be sold, and therefore often not included in many lockup percentages.

What will happen in December when an additional 17% of SpaceX's float becomes publicly traded? History has a clear answer.

When SpaceX went public, many analysts were worried that ongoing share unlocks could create selling pressure for the space stock.

In October, SpaceX experienced the largest share unlock of any company in history, freeing up more than $180 billion worth of stock. As analysts predicted, volatility ensued. Early in the month, shares zoomed from $150 to $170, only to shed those gains by the time earnings were announced on Oct. 21. When 1.3 billion shares were unlocked on Oct. 22, heavy supply pressure resulted in a 9% single-day drop. By the end of October, SpaceX's stock price was below its initial IPO price.

The largest unlock period for SpaceX before that was in early August. Roughly $100 billion in shares became available for trading. Shares initially dropped below $110 as the unlock date approached. Afterward, however, shares rallied, ending the month with a 32% gain.

In general, there's no guarantee what will happen before, during, or after a major share unlock. If there were a strong trend, markets -- which are forward-looking -- would quickly price that upward or downward potential into the stock's current valuation. That's especially true given that the market knows exactly when unlock dates will be triggered. Some research suggests that a 1% to 3% drop during an unlocking event is to be expected on average, but that's an incredibly rough estimate for a single security.

In short, investors are likely wise not to trade around SpaceX's lockup period, even if it is one of the largest share unlocks in history. The better move is likely to assess the projected fair value of SpaceX as a business and accumulate shares if selling pressure arises and pushes the company's stock price below your fair value estimate.

Share unlock schedules get a lot of media attention. But in the long term, they mean very little. Even though SpaceX's unlock schedule is historic, expect these events to have minimal impact on the company's long-term success.

Ryan Vanzo has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3097. DraftKings: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

The self-funded push into predictions rests on how quickly new customers pay back their acquisition cost, which the article examines closely.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 6, 2026, DraftKings (DKNG -21.30%) announced an increased 2026 adjusted EBITDA investment in its predictions business of $650 million to $700 million to capture rapid customer growth. This aggressive push into prediction markets alters short-term profitability while creating a low-volatility revenue stream. Understanding how capital gets allocated between core sports betting and new product lines is vital to evaluating the company's long-term earnings trajectory. Three key takeaways from management's comments highlight the strategic direction.

Management expanded capital deployment into prediction markets because customer acquisition metrics indicated faster cost recovery than originally projected. Quick payback cycles mitigate potential legal risks around event contracts while expanding the user base across non-sports categories.

DraftKings relies on its core sportsbook and gaming operations to generate cash, allowing it to fund new ventures internally without taking on debt or issuing shares. Higher net revenue margins and disciplined overhead costs support this self-funded expansion strategy.

Investments in proprietary game content and application updates accelerated user acquisition in the digital casino segment. Management expects these initial promotional expenditures to convert into expanded gross profit margins as player cohorts mature.

DraftKings is expanding its revenue base through cross-selling across its digital sports and predictions platform, setting up top-line expansion for fiscal 2027.

Integrated technology ownership and direct marketing discipline give the business long-term pricing power while management monitors state regulatory developments and capital allocation rules.

#3095. Forget Lilly and Novo: Pfizer Is Planning a Wave of Phase 3 Obesity Trials

Everyone is talking about the GLP-1 battle between Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk, but Pfizer wants in on the fight, too.

Eli Lilly (LLY -0.79%) is much loved on Wall Street because of its highly successful GLP-1 weight-loss business. Novo Nordisk (NVO +0.03%) is much less appreciated because it lost the GLP-1 lead to Eli Lilly. But they aren't the only companies competing in this still-developing drug category. Pfizer (PFE -0.20%), which is deeply out of favor among investors, could be a surprise contender as it advances its GLP-1 drugs through the approval process. Here's what you need to know.

Eli Lilly's stock is up more than 420% over the past five years, as of this writing. While it was second to market with a GLP-1 weight-loss drug, it quickly overtook Novo Nordisk's lead. Being passed by Eli Lilly hasn't gone over well with investors, which is why Novo Nordisk's stock is down 27% over the past five years. However, Novo Nordisk's GLP-1 pill has been performing well, so there's still hope for the business.

Left out in the cold has been Pfizer. Despite being a highly respected pharmaceutical company, Pfizer's stock is down nearly 30% over the past five years. A big part of the problem is that Pfizer's internally developed GLP-1 drug was dropped, which was a huge black eye for the company. Investors may be taking too dim a view of the future here.

Competition in the healthcare sector is intense, and innovation doesn't always work out as hoped. Well-run companies know how to deal with both issues. This is why it is important to note that Pfizer quickly turned around and acquired a company with an attractive GLP-1 drug still in development.

Buying a drug that's still in development isn't a guarantee of success. So the company still has a lot of work to do, but it is work that Pfizer knows well. The company now has multiple clinical trials going on in the weight-loss space. The key to Pfizer's approach is convenience.

Currently, Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly GLP-1 weight-loss drugs are taken either weekly by injection or daily by pill. Pfizer is looking to offer patients a monthly shot option. In recent trials, Pfizer's GLP-1 drug helped to reduce weight by 12% even after shifting to monthly shots. Patients don't like taking shots, so selling a GLP-1 shot with a monthly cadence may be highly desirable. Once-a-month dosing may even beat daily pills, since it means remembering to take medicine far less often. The power of convenience can't be overstated.

All in, Pfizer has 10 late-stage trials in its weight-loss portfolio. Each of those trials will help bring it closer to having an approved GLP-1 product to sell. But the real investment story here isn't the trials; it's that Wall Street appears to have written Pfizer off as an also-ran. That's particularly notable for dividend investors, given the stock's lofty 5.5% yield. The average pharma stock yields 1.3%, while Eli Lilly's yield is even lower at 0.5%. Novo Nordisk's yield is roughly 4.4%, which is attractive but not as high as Pfizer's.

Maintaining and growing the dividend over time are key priorities for Pfizer. While it is working from behind right now, the company's long history of success in the drug space suggests it will eventually turn things around. And the late-stage GLP-1 trials it has underway strongly suggest that the inflection point could be closer than Wall Street currently believes. If you are a long-term dividend investor, now could be a good time to get to know Pfizer. The yield may not be as attractive if you wait until the company actually enters the GLP-1 race with an approved product.

Reuben Gregg Brewer has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Eli Lilly, Novo Nordisk, and Pfizer. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3094. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3090. Consumer Staples Showdown: Weighing the Differences Between VDC and RSPS

Vanguard delivered 21.3% returns over one year with lower costs, while Invesco's equal-weighting approach offers higher dividend income at 2.8%.

The Vanguard Consumer Staples ETF (VDC -1.16%) offers broad sector exposure with very low costs, while the Invesco S&P 500 Equal Weight Consumer Staples ETF (RSPS -1.34%) equally weights its holdings to reduce concentration risk.

Both exchange-traded funds provide a defensive anchor for portfolios by targeting non-discretionary goods, yet they differ sharply in construction. One relies on market-capitalization weighting, which can lead to top-heavy positions, whereas the other ensures that every company in the index carries an equal influence regardless of its market value.

Beta measures price volatility relative to the S&P 500; beta is calculated from monthly returns over the available fund history (up to five years). The 1-yr return represents total return over the trailing 12 months. Dividend yield is the trailing-12-month distribution yield.

The Vanguard fund is the more affordable choice for long-term investors with its 0.09% expense ratio. While the Invesco fund is more expensive at 0.4%, it offers a noticeably higher payout for income-focused investors.

The Vanguard Consumer Staples ETF holds 103 positions, providing broad exposure, with 98% of the portfolio concentrated in the consumer defensive sector. Its largest holdings include Walmart Inc (WMT -1.72%) at 13.86%, Costco Wholesale Corp (COST -1.13%) at 12.12%, and Procter & Gamble Co. (PG -1.02%) at 9.90%. It was launched in 2004 and manages its portfolio without specific quirks. Vanguard Consumer Staples ETF has paid $4.88 per share over the trailing 12 months, which, on its recent ~$246.9 share price, works out to a 2% yield.

The Invesco S&P 500 Equal Weight Consumer Staples ETF is more focused, with 34 holdings, and employs an equal-weighting strategy to prevent any single stock from dominating the fund. Its portfolio is 94% consumer defensive, with 3% in both consumer cyclical and basic materials. Its largest positions include Kimberly-Clark Corp (KMB -1.84%) at 5.47%, Kroger Co. (KR -2.49%) at 3.33%, and Walmart at 3.32%. It was launched in 2006. Invesco S&P 500 Equal Weight Consumer Staples ETF has paid $0.89 per share over the trailing 12 months, which, on its recent ~$32.0 share price, works out to a 2.8% yield.

For more guidance on ETF investing, check out the full guide at this link.

When comparing the Vanguard Consumer Staples ETF (VDC) and the Invesco S&P 500 Equal Weight Consumer Staples ETF (RSPS), investors should consider several key factors. Let's see what they tell us about each fund.

First, we should examine each fund's strategy. VDC is a market-weighted fund that includes consumer staples stocks from the large-cap, mid-cap, and small-cap segments. RSPS, on the other hand, is an equal-weighted fund that includes consumer staples stocks from the S&P 500. VDC holds about 100 stocks, while RSPS holds far fewer, around 40 in all.

Two other important factors are historical performance and income potential. Over the last five years, VDC has generated a total return of 45%, equating to a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 7.7%. RSPS, by contrast, has delivered a total return of 14%, with a 2.7% CAGR. Both funds have underperformed the S&P 500 over this period. As for income, RSPS delivers the higher yield, at 2.8% versus only 2% for VDC.

One final factor to weigh is cost. Here, VDC gains an advantage. Its 0.09% annual expense ratio is far lower than RSPS's 0.40%. For context, if a person were to invest $10,000 in both funds, they should expect to pay $9 in annual fees for their VDC shares but $40 for their RSPS shares.In summary, VDC and RSPS both hold consumer staples stocks, but there are important differences in their structures. Many investors will favor VDC given its lower fees and superior historical returns. However, those seeking an equal-weighted approach or higher yields may still consider RSPS.

Jake Lerch has positions in Procter & Gamble. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Costco Wholesale and Walmart. The Motley Fool recommends Kroger. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3087. American Healthcare REIT: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Relationship-driven sourcing lets American Healthcare REIT buy newer infill senior housing without bidding against rivals in public auctions.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 6, 2026, American Healthcare REIT (AHR +3.34%) announced an increase to its full-year normalized funds from operations (NFFO) guidance, a cash-flow performance metric for real estate investment trusts. For long-term investors, operational efficiency across senior housing properties is driving compounding cash flows. Three things management said stand out.

In the Senior Housing Operating Portfolio (SHOP), where American Healthcare REIT retains direct operational exposure, revenue per occupied room grew much faster than operating expenses per room. That widening spread expanded operating margins and drove substantially higher same-store net operating income (NOI)--property cash flow after operating costs--across the segment.

Rather than competing in public auctions where asset prices are bid up, management relies on direct relationships with operators to acquire properties. This off-market strategy allows the real estate investment trust (REIT) to buy newer senior housing communities in supply-constrained infill markets without overpaying.

Management reduced net debt relative to earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA), expanding its funding capacity. By match-funding acquisitions through unsettled forward equity sales, the company supports its pipeline while preserving financial flexibility.

Aging demographics provide structural growth tailwinds for senior housing communities. Management relies on proprietary technology tools and regional operator partnerships to turn sector fundamentals into operational execution.

Capital deployment remains focused on disciplined match-funding for acquisitions in high-barrier markets while maintaining low leverage.

#3074. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

Luka Dončić's hot start to the season received an early MVP endorsement from an opposing coach.

Trail Blazers coach Micah Nori had high praise for the Lakers superstar ahead of Friday's matchup between the Lakers and Trail Blazers at Crypto.com Arena.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers handles the ball against the Portland Trail Blazers on Friday, Nov. 6, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

Dončić entered Friday with averages of 38.5 points on 49.5% shooting (43.1% on 3-pointers), 8.1 rebounds, eight assists and 1.8 steals.

Portland Trail Blazers head coach Micah Nori yells to players during an NBA basketball game against the Los Angeles Lakers in Los Angeles, Tuesday, Oct. 27, 2026. AP Photo/Caroline Brehman

"This is the best shape I've ever seen him in," Nori said of Dončić pregame.
