#2993. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2992. The race is on to build the first fusion power plant — and Europe is looking to Japan for help

The race is on to build the world's first commercial fusion power plant — and one of Europe's leading contenders in the field has just received a boost.

Munich-based Proxima Fusion signed memoranda of understanding with two Japanese industrial partners, Fujikura and Kyoto Fusioneering, in Tokyo on Oct. 28. Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi and German Chancellor Friedrich Merz — who was visiting in a bid to deepen trade ties — were both in attendance.

Ann Mettler, president of Catalyse Europe, a Brussels-based clean-energy think tank, and a former vice president of Breakthrough Energy, said developing the technology should be a strategic imperative for Europe.

"Europe is a resource-poor geography and we are very dependent on China. The world is in competition for technological supremacy — this is a zero-sum game," Mettler told CNBC, speaking in a personal capacity.

"Fusion is a silver bullet because the promise is abundant, clean, limitless energy."

Fusion energy seeks to replicate the way the sun produces power, using super-heated plasma to fuse two light atomic nuclei into one heavier one, releasing vast amounts of energy in the process.

There are two main approaches: one uses high-powered lasers, while the other uses powerful magnets to confine the plasma. Proxima is developing a type of magnetic device called a stellarator, which is designed to hold the plasma in place for long periods.

The agreement with Kyoto Fusioneering will expand existing work on next-generation gyrotrons — devices that generate high-power microwaves used to heat the plasma.

Proxima's agreement with 141-year-old Fujikura will explore plans for industrial-scale manufacturing in Germany of high-temperature superconducting, or HTS, tape, a specialized metallic tape that is superconducting at relatively high temperatures and can carry electrical current with virtually no resistive losses.

The tape is wound into coils to form powerful magnets that confine and control the heated plasma inside the stellarator.

Francesco Sciortino, CEO and co-founder of Proxima Fusion, told CNBC that Fujikura, which employs over 50,000 people worldwide, was a leader in manufacturing high-temperature superconductors, having built its reputation in optical fibers. "They think this tape has the potential to be the next optical fibre," he said.

He said Proxima, which is studying plans to build a manufacturing facility in Lower Saxony, hoped to make around 5,000 kilometers, or 3,100 miles, of tape annually by 2030.

The 140 million euro ($160 million) facility would be financed partly by the proceeds of a $468 million funding round in July, which valued Proxima, whose backers include Alphabet-owned Google, at $2.7 billion.

Proxima expects to need around 20,000 kilometers (12,400 miles) of the tape for its "Alpha" fusion demonstrator and a further 40,000 kilometers (24,900 miles) for its planned "Stellaris" fusion power station.

Sciortino said that establishing production of the tape in Germany was crucial as it would help Europe create the expertise and supply chains needed to turn its fusion research leadership into industrial capability.

This is where Europe has historically struggled, according to Catalyse Europe's Mettler.

"Every geography wants to turn leadership in science into industrial leadership and this is where Europe has traditionally been not so good — turning that scientific leadership into euros," she said.

While Europe had industrial giants to be proud of, disruptors tended to be more successful in frontier technologies, according to Mettler.

"If Europe is first to develop this technology, it will give us a huge advantage, but key to that will be building the supply chain," she said.

Sciortino said he remains confident Proxima will be the first company to turn on a pilot plant — but stressed the key to profitability would be making that plant as simple as possible to operate.

"You can have complexity in the design, manufacturing and operation of the plant," he said. "We can take care of the complexity in the design and manufacturing, but our view is that there needs to be simplicity in the operation. We are aiming for net energy gain during the early 2030s."

But Proxima has some well-heeled competitors. They include U.S.-based Commonwealth Fusion Systems, which is also backed by Google, and Helion Energy, as well as Canada's General Fusion. China has a number of state-backed players, most notably China Fusion Energy Co., while Tokyo-based Helical Fusion leads Japan's push.

"The supply chain in fusion energy needs to be built years before — people often think of frontier technologies as a switch that can be thrown," Mettler said. "That is not the case here."

#2989. Utah Jazz vs Dallas Mavericks: Recap and Final Score

Utah's losing streak extended to seven games after losing to the Dallas Mavericks, 112-100.

The Jazz started this game pretty well overall and showed impressive defense on second-year phenom Cooper Flagg. Ace Bailey deserves a lot of the credit for keeping Flagg to 21 points. Utah also did a good job on Kyrie Irving, holding him to just 35% from the field. Keyonte George has shown solid defense this season, and his defense showed up tonight in his moments guarding Irving and others. It's a great sign for the Jazz's future that he's developing that way. Combine that with Ace Bailey's defense and the emerging play of Darryn Peterson, and the Jazz have something brewing. It's just hard to see when you're in the midst of a prolonged losing streak.

The second half was different for Utah. In the third quarter, the Mavericks overcame the Jazz starters and forced substitutions. That's when Isaiah Collier, Svi Mykhailiuk, and Brice Sensabaugh got minutes again. This bench unit has been bad all season and keeps getting minutes, even after Isaiah Collier's contract wasn't re-upped for next season. Will Hardy is in a tough place with Jaren Jackson Jr. out, and you don't want to run a 7-man lineup all season, but something has to be done to stop these Collier/Sensabaugh/Mykhailiuk minutes. It's costing the Jazz wins night after night.

That said, Hardy deserves credit for some of his adjustments. He's staggered lineups to keep it from being a completely all-bench unit. Tonight it was with Lauri Markkanen in that lineup, but it didn't work. The issue with Markkanen is that you can't just give him the ball and expect him to go get a bucket, he's a purely off-ball player. What should theoretically work is having Keyonte George and Darryn Peterson stagger minutes to make sure one of them is on the floor at all times. Darryn Peterson might not have been quite ready for that to start the season, although I think that's debatable, but after tonight, he's looking more and more ready for a bigger role.

The good in this game was the duo of Keyonte George and Darryn Peterson. Peterson had one of his best statistical performances, shooting 50/42.9/100 with 21 points, 5 assists, and 2 rebounds. Peterson looked more comfortable in the fourth quarter, and you can only hope that it's the beginning of him taking a leap.

Keyonte George also continues a mostly awesome season. He had 16 points, 6 rebounds, and 9 assists in this one. George's production is great, he just needs to cut down on turnovers. If he can keep this production and cut down on the bad turnovers, he's looking at becoming one of the best guards in the league.

Josh² has been a great addition to the team. Josh Green and Josh Okogie have brought great two-way play for the Jazz and are playing well off of the Jazz's young guards. There's a formula here that should work for the Jazz.

The downside is the bench unit, specifically Svi Mykhailiuk, Isaiah Collier, and Brice Sensabaugh. It hasn't worked all season and has cost the Jazz multiple wins. What's even more frustrating is how quickly Will Hardy has been to pull Peterson after a mistake. The issue is that the players he replaces them with aren't playing well or mistake-free themselves. Collier had three turnovers in this one, and a few came from travel calls. That bench unit also gave up the biggest lead and pretty much ended the Jazz's chance at a win. Hardy is in a tough spot missing Jaren Jackson Jr. with a very young team, so there's almost no room for error. If there is one thing that Utah could really use, though, is fewer minutes from those three. If they can give Josh² more minutes, that should help. But even with that, it's going to be tough to get wins.

Finally, Utah is ready for Ace Bailey's threes to start dropping. He's in a big slump right now, and when he comes out of it, it will be huge for the team. His defensive impact is great, and his rebounding is fantastic. Once his three-point shooting comes around, he'll help the Jazz get more win streaks than losing streaks.

#2987. Mississippi Senate: Longshot Ty Pinkins says he aimed to spoil Hyde-Smith, boost Colom vote

Longshot Mississippi Senate candidate Ty Pinkins said that at first wanted to win — but that he stayed in to spoil.

Pinkins told a local TV station that after an April poll showed he had no chance of winning he remained in the race in order to boost Democratic nominee Scott Colom's chances of beating Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins' purported strategy might pay off.

Colom is narrowly leading Hyde-Smith in ongoing counting by just 1,936 votes, a margin of just 0.2 percentage points, with 96.2% of the vote counted, according to the Associated Press. That margin is about one-tenth the size of Pinkins' share of the vote, 2.1 percentage points.

Pinkins has won just 20,010 votes so far out of the nearly 952,000 votes already counted.

"The only thing I did was stay in the race and let people shoot arrows at me," Pinkins said in an interview with WTVA in Mississippi on Thursday. "That is it. And that's a ... low price to pay to get someone in this race that I think can help people in the Delta and all across this state."

"The portion of the vote that I drew — and people were concerned about splitting the Black vote — what I actually did was split the Republican vote," he said.

Pinkins told the station in Tupelo that he intentionally avoided campaigning in areas with large Black populations to avoid cutting into the vote share of Colom, who, like Pinkins, is Black.

That tactic worked, he said, as the election results show he got most of his support from areas with relatively low Black populations.

"I'll be the boogeyman," he said. "But I'll tell you this: The independent in this race pulled most of his votes from a Republican, and for the first time since Reconstruction, a Democrat who is a Black man is leading."

Pinkins, a lawyer who previously ran for Mississippi secretary of state, was the Democratic nominee for Senate in 2024. He was crushed by Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, who won nearly 63% of the vote, compared with about 37% for Pinkins.

Despite that showing, the U.S. Army veteran decided to take another crack at the Senate in 2026.

But in the spring, he saw the writing on the wall for his candidacy.

"I got in this race to win," Pinkins told WTVA. "In April, I knew I was going to lose, but I also knew that I was pulling 70% of the Republican vote based on the [SPLC Action Fund and Impact Research] poll."

SPLC Action Fund, when it released the poll, said, "Sagging favorability numbers for Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith could open up a path to victory for Democrats."

"Independent Ty Pinkins has the potential to play spoiler for either candidate," SPLC Action Fund said in April.

In the WTVA interview, Pinkins said, "I probably attended maybe, maybe six events over the summer" as part of his campaign.

"Six events. I don't have to," Pinkins said. "Why? I'm not going to win. So why go to these Black communities that he's going to?"

Pinkins said all he had to do was visit counties that have relatively low Black populations and "talk to some Republicans that said they're going to vote for me."

"They were literally calling me and sending me emails: 'We're going to vote for you because we don't want to waste our vote on Cindy Hyde-Smith, we don't want to hold our nose and vote for her, so we're going to vote for you. Please stay in the race,'" he said.

The campaigns of Colom and Hyde-Smith did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

#2986. Chiefs-Jets: 5 questions with Gang Green Nation ahead of Week 9

On Sunday, the Kansas City Chiefs face the New York Jets in the early afternoon slot of Week 9 games. We welcome MacGregor Wells of Gang Green Nation— our sister SBNation site covering the Jets— for Five Questions with the Enemy.

Join fellow Chiefs fans to react to every explosive play and game-changing moment in real time.

Download the SB Nation app on iOS or Android.

Download the SB Nation app on your mobile device and join the Gang Green Nation community through Sunday's game to get perspectives from both sides of the Week 9 matchup!

Part of this is always luck. Which team wins a one-score game often comes down to an untimely turnover, a costly penalty, an unlucky bounce, whatever. Things that are going to happen to any team, but happen at the wrong time to losing teams. However, the element that isn't just bad luck for the Jets is bad coaching. The Jets have blown fairly big second-half leads. They have had comebacks come up just short. They have outplayed better teams only to lose late, and they have come out flat against lesser teams they should have beaten. The one stat that people keep on bringing up is a 100 – 6 deficit in the last two minutes of halves under Aaron Glenn's "leadership." I think the numbers have changed slightly since that stat came out, but you get the picture.

That kind of absurd imbalance in two-minute situations is the direct result of poor coaching. The Jets look completely lost in two-minute drills. How can an NFL team be that clueless and that ineffective in crunch time? It's nuts. There are plenty of other coaching issues I could mention, but that one stat kind of encapsulates the general incompetence of the Jets coaching.

It's frustrating because for so many years the Jets simply didn't have the talent to be competitive. This year they have the talent. Oh, don't get me wrong, this is no juggernaut roster. But unlike recent years, the Jets at least have the talent to be competitive in almost every game. They have been competitive. But they continue to lose. Thank you, Aaron Glenn.

Part of this is probably just good luck. Smith has always been a turnover-prone quarterback. He began this season with five straight interception-free games, but it seemed like a mirage. His judgment and field vision haven't suddenly taken a quantum leap in the twilight of his career. Sure enough, Smith has thrown an interception in each of his last three games. Reversion to the mean is a powerful thing.

The other part is the talent that surrounds Smith on offense. The Jets have a good offensive line. They have multiple talented backs. They have solid tight ends, and they have three good receivers in Garrett Wilson, Adonai Mitchell, and rookie Omar Cooper. The defense is a mess, but the offensive talent is the best group the Jets have had in at least a decade. Geno Smith has benefited from the talent around him. Even so, the Jets have scored more than 25 points in a game just once all year, so for all the somewhat gaudy Geno stats, the bottom line is the Jets haven't exactly lit up the scoreboard.

Third-year running back Braelon Allen has been impressive since Breece Hall exited game three with a leg injury, thrusting Allen into the starting role. In the last five games, Allen has been one of the best running backs in the NFL. Hall is likely to finally be back in the lineup for the Jets on Sunday, but I'm hoping, probably in vain, that the Jets have seen that Allen is the better back.

Regardless of which back gets the bulk of the work, the Chiefs have been one of the best teams in the NFL in stopping the run, so if the Jets are to have any chance in this game, I would guess Geno Smith and the Jets receivers will need to have a big game. Omar Cooper might be the guy to look out for, as well as fellow rookie tight end Kenyon Sadiq. If Garrett Wilson doesn't play, those two are the most talented weapons the Jets have in the passing game.

The Jets' 11th ranking in total defense is a sham. The Jets defense stinks. They have one good pass rusher, rookie David Bailey. The interior defensive line is a shambles with both David Onyemata and T'Vondre Sweat out injured. The linebackers are slow and mostly ineffective. The same can be said about the cornerbacks. Safety Dane Belton has been a revelation in his first year with the Jets after spending his early career with the Giants. He's been really good, especially against the run. The other safety, Minkah Fitzpatrick, is in steep decline, living on reputation after a stellar career.

This is an untalented Jets defense that had the good fortune to face some bad offenses, some backup quarterbacks and some teams with injury problems early in the year. Lately, the results have deteriorated badly. The Jets have allowed 26+ points on defense in each of the last three games. I expect that to be more of what we can expect from the Jets defense going forward.

This shouldn't be a challenging game for the Kansas City offense. I expect Kenneth Walker to run wild against a porous Jets run defense, and I expect Patrick Mahomes to pick apart a mediocre Jets secondary. If the Jets have any chance for an unlikely upset on Sunday, it will probably have to be in a shootout. I just don't see the Jets defense shutting down Mahomes, Walker, Rice and company.

The Jets have one chance against the Chiefs: a Chiefs letdown. It isn't always easy getting up for the dregs of the league. Sometimes you look past a lesser opponent. If the Chiefs come out flat, maybe the Jets can steal a win if the Jets offense plays up to its talent level. However, Kansas City is a very difficult venue for Chiefs opponents. Even if the Chiefs come out flat, it's likely the Jets' bad coaching will blow the opportunity for an upset. The gap between future Hall of Fame coach Andy Reid and future out-of-work coach Aaron Glenn is enormous. It will be difficult for the Jets to overcome that.

If the Chiefs don't come out flat, this game shouldn't be close. I am terrible at score predictions, but let's go with Chiefs 27, Jets 16.

#2982. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.

#2980. Lakers’ Ziaire Williams confuses Luka Doncic with Austin Reaves: ‘wrong white person’

Ziaire Williams approached Austin Reaves from behind during his walk-off interview with NBA on Prime, put his hands on his shoulders and yelled, "Fifty ball."

It was Luka Dončić who had scored 53 points in the Lakers' 124-118 win over the Trail Blazers on Friday night at Crypto.com Arena.

"I'm the wrong white person," Reaves, who had 25 points, retorted as he shook his head in disbelief.

Did Williams seriously confuse his two teammates?

"Deadass," Williams said, flashing a smile in the Lakers' locker room.

Ziaire Williams celebrates after a bucket during the Lakers' 124-118 comeback win over the Trail Blazers on Nov. 6, 2026 in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Mark J. Terrill

Luka Doncic (left) and Austin Reaves look on during the Lakers' win over the Raptors on Nov. 1, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

#2976. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2975. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs. Portland Trail Blazers

The San Antonio Spurs are on a roll. The Spurs extended their winning streak to seven games after defeating the Sacramento Kings 124-109 on Friday. Now they'll face one of the NBA's hottest teams, the Portland Trail Blazers. It will be the first time these two teams meet since the Spurs defeated the Blazers in the first round of last year's playoffs.

Portland looks a bit different than they did last season. Damian Lillard is back from an Achilles injury, averaging 20.1 points per game. Deni Advija continues to be one of the league's underrated stars. The Blazers lost a close one, 124-118, to the Los Angeles Lakers on Friday night, but have so far been one of the best teams in the league.

Both teams will be in the second game of a back-to-back, so fatigue will be a consideration. The Spurs have shown they have a solid game plan for the Blazers, but with both teams on short rest, this one could go either way. A win over a red-hot Portland squad would be a signature win in the early stretch of the season.

Spurs Injuries: To be released at 1 p.m. CT

Trail Blazers Injuries: To be released at 1 p.m. CT

The Blazers have the best offensive rating in the NBA (121.9). That's thanks in large part to their ability to get into the paint and then convert on kick-out three-pointers. Advija is one of the best downhill players in the NBA. Toumani Camara is one of the best spot-up shooters in the league. As a whole, Portland makes the fifth most three-pointers per game at 15.4. The Spurs are faced with a tough challenge – stop the Blazers from getting easy shots at the rim and close out hard on shooters.

It feels like the Blazers' identity for the last few seasons has been a hard-nosed, physical squad. Donovan Clingan is one of the best rebounding bigs in the league, and Robert Williams III causes a lot of problems with his motor and athleticism. Then they have physical wings like Jrue Holiday and Advija who are problems on the glass. The Spurs have not been truly tested in the rebounding battle this season. But with a smaller, guard-filled lineup, San Antonio has to focus on winning the battle on the boards to win this game.

San Antonio hit 50% of their threes against the Kings on Friday. The Spurs need those shots to fall to open up their downhill attacking offense. The Blazers will likely put Clingan on Stephon Castle, daring him to make threes, just like they did in the playoffs last season. San Antonio can offset this by using him as a screener or putting the ball in his hands in the pick-and-roll to get Clingan moving around on the perimeter. Regardless of defensive scheme, the Spurs will have to hit three-point shots to come away with the win in Portland.

#2974. Miami Dolphins offensive line review: Tracking the guards in Week 8

Despite a glorious halftime lead and a legitimate chance to pull off a second consecutive win against a divisional opponent, the Dolphins faltered against the Patriots last Sunday. Still, we probably learn more from losses than wins, and this remains a season focused on learning and development.

That is especially true along the offensive line, where rookie left guard Kadyn Proctor and second-year right guard Jonah Savaiinaea are important pieces of the team's future. How are they performing this season? Each week, we take a closer look at the guards and what is being said about their most recent performance.

The first thing we learned is that the offensive line was out of sorts without Kadyn Proctor.

Maybe it was a coincidence, the result of good defensive scheming or an intentionally deflated football (#neverforget), but the offensive line never seemed to operate as a cohesive unit against the Patriots. And the stats back that up. Not that sacks and run efficiency tell the whole story, but let's look at the team performance since Week 4, when Ollie Gordon first took over as the featured running back:

Week 4: Gordon, 9 rushes, 100 yards (11.1 ypc); 2 sacks allowed

Week 5: Gordon, 18 rushes, 88 yards (4.9 ypc); 3 sacks allowed

Week 7 following the bye: Gordon, 25 rushes, 103 yards (4.1 ypc); 2 sacks allowed

Week 8: Gordon, 19 rushes, 69 yards (3.6 ypc); 5 sacks allowed

As a team, the Dolphins' yards-per-carry was a season-low 3.2 last Sunday.

How much of that dip in performance is due to Proctor's absence? It's hard to say for sure, but I'm sure we're all happy Proctor has cleared concussion protocol and is back in the lineup against the Lions.

As for Jonah Savaiinaea, this was also a game to forget. Not only did he pick up his first penalty of the season (a holding call in the second quarter), but his overall PFF grade was the lowest of this week's five starters on the offensive line, including Proctor's replacement, Jarrett Kingston. Just looking at the PFF trends above, after his two best games in September against the 49ers and Chiefs, he's turned in below-average grades in each of his last four appearances. Even if you're not a believer in PFF grading, OL Coach Eric Smith in his weekly YouTube offensive line review (see the link below) also noted that Savaiinaea's play has fallen off since September, and that his performance last week was reminiscent of 2025, when Jonah was statistically one of the worst guards in the league.

My theory is that because the lines (offensive and defensive) function more as a unit, their performance is going to be impacted more by the absence of a starter than, say, the wide receiver room. I guess we can put that theory to the test this Sunday.

The tracker will be updated each week throughout the season. For a more in-depth breakdown of Proctor's and Savaiinaea's performance, check out offensive line Coach Eric Smith's film analysis of the game, which you can find on his YouTube channel, Focused on Fins.

#2973. Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

#2971. Investors brace for lower Anthropic valuation, calling $1.5 trillion a 'stretch'

Following tech's selloff on Wall Street this week, some investors are lowering their expectations for Anthropic.

The artificial intelligence company had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation in its IPO up until recently, but stakeholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

The change in sentiment comes alongside growing concerns about the outsized spending by AI companies and a sharp post-midterm slump in the market. The Nasdaq plummeted more than 5% on Thursday, its steepest drop in 19 months, reflecting fears that Democrats' newfound power in Washington, D.C., and a nationwide rebuke of AI will lead to a slowdown in the buildout of data centers.

Bearishness on the AI trade was on the rise before the election. The Nasdaq suffered its worst month in October since March 2025, led by declines in chipmakers and neoclouds.

Anthropic is set to list on the Nasdaq, under the ticker ANT, and is gearing up for an expected debut in a matter of weeks. Last month, Anthropic was reportedly seeking a valuation as high as $2 trillion. As of May, the company was privately valued at $965 billion.

With the latest market turmoil, one investor said $1 trillion to $1.25 trillion is a more likely valuation target, although that could change with market conditions. The investors asked not to be named because they're not authorized to speak about the matter.

Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC that, "Investors will expect the IPO to be priced at least at the low end of previous expectations."

"The market really needs this IPO to go well and the bankers on the deal surely understand that," Luria said. "The best way to set Anthropic up for success is to price the deal well."

The company is expected to kick off its roadshow next week.

#2970. Spurs blow out lowly Kings in first NBA Cup game of the season

With the Spurs on a six-game win streak and the Kangz being, well, the Kangz, the good guys unsurprisingly walked away with a 124-109 victory on Friday night.

Both sides traded blows from the tip, energized to play their first NBA Cup match of the season. It didn't take long, though, for San Antonio to pull ahead, as the guards took advantage of Sacramento's lack of interior defense (or defense, period), to drive to the basket or lob it up to Wemby for easy dunks. However, in typical Spurs fashion, they took their foot off the gas and let the Kings go on a mini run to get back in the game. Soon after, San Antonio became more intentional with their offensive sets, running some nice plays that used Vassell's gravity and passing ability to find open cutters. The Spurs reasserted their dominance in the game.

Still, Wemby had a fall just before halftime that made everyone hold their breath. The big fella landed on Domantas Sabonis' foot on his way down from shooting a three, grabbing his ankle while rolling on the court. He appeared to be in quite some discomfort and stayed down for a minute before walking to the Spurs bench on his own power, seemingly able to shake it off. It marked the end of a dominant first half for the Alien, as he finished with 19 points in two quarters alone.

Thankfully, Wemby's extraterrestrial abilities seem to have extended to his healing abilities. He started the second half and was no worse for wear, although the two teams came out flat to start the third. The offense on both ends was sloppy and stagnant, forcing Mitch Johnson to call a timeout when the Kings cut the lead down to 14. The message was heard once again; the good guys locked back in and began moving the ball more, finding open shooters who converted on their free looks. Garbage time began early in the fourth, with both teams emptying their benches. San Antonio cruised to a comfortable 124-109 victory, securing their first NBA Cup win of the season.

Wemby finished the night after logging just 26 minutes, putting up a dominant stat line of 30 points, 14 rebounds, and 2 blocks. After a slow start to the season, he's playing arguably the best basketball of his career in the last two weeks, and this might be a sign that he's leveling up even more. The ankle is something that should be monitored, though, as swelling often happens in the days after the initial injury. Wemby had a similar-looking ankle roll in his rookie season that forced him to miss a few games after finishing the contest in which the injury occurred.

The Spurs were fortunate that they played Sacramento, allowing Wemby to play fewer minutes after his ankle tweak. Remember, point differential counts in the NBA Cup, so teams oftentimes play their stars big minutes even if a win is guaranteed. San Antonio sits second in Group C following Friday's victory, as their +15 differential is just behind Golden State's +17.

The game was so out of reach that the backups' backups got some playing time. Carter Bryant, David Jones Garcia, and Tarris Reed all checked in with 3 minutes to go. Barnesy had a hot shooting night too, pouring in 11 points in under 20 minutes of action. Hindsight is always 20/20, but I do wonder if Mitch should've stuck with the usual backups. San Antonio was up by over 20 points before the third stringers checked in, and they might've finished with a higher point differential than Golden State's had they stuck with the second unit.

The Kornet minutes remain an issue, even against the Kangz. The veteran finished as a -10 in just 18 minutes, while Wemby was a +31 in 26 minutes. I'm honestly not sure which stat is more impressive.

It's just the Kangz, Steph! They're already dead!

The Spurs will look to make it 8 in a row as they face off against the hot 6-1 Blazers on Saturday. Tip-off is once again at 9pm CST (sigh).

#2968. World’s No. 1 restaurant crowned as contest comes under increasing scrutiny

Asador Etxebarri in Atxondo, Spain, is the world's top restaurant.

That's according to The World's 50 Best Restaurants, which announced results on Wednesday evening in Lima, Peru. Asador Etxebarri, helmed by chef Bittor Arguinzoniz, sits in Basque Country and features grilled items like a charred beef chop, fresh mozzarella from the chef's buffalo, or house-made chorizo.

Chef Gaggan Anand's Gaggan in Bangkok, which combines progressive Indian cuisine with French, Thai and Japanese influences, was No. 2, and Alchemist in Copenhagen, from head chef Rasmus Munk, took the third spot.

India returned to the top 50 for the first time since 2004, with Masque at No. 26 and NAAR at No. 49. Mainland China made the list for the first time since 2021, with Meet the Bund in Shanghai at No. 21 and Ru Yuan in Hangzhou at No. 36.

Chef-restaurateur Ferran Adrià, who achieved worldwide renown with his avant-garde techniques at his now-closed restaurant El Bulli, received the SevenRooms Icon Award. Adria continues to support culinary research and education through the nonprofit El Bulli Foundation.

The 50, as the umbrella organization is called, creates lists of restaurants, bars, hotels and vineyards that it ranks as top in the world or region. It draws on industry networks to help rank establishments.

For years, The 50 has been seen as a kingmaker and an opportunity for restaurants and bars worldwide. Being "on the list," many chefs and other restaurant-industry insiders have said, makes a huge difference for reservations and revenues.

But there's been increased scrutiny of the contest as well. Chef Ana Roš of Hiša Franko in Slovenia, which was ranked 60th this year, posted on Instagram that she wouldn't be attending the Lima ceremony, saying she would rather invest resources "in our guests, our team, our producers and the daily work of making the restaurant better."

While stressing that she had enjoyed a "vibrant community" at the 50 Best and made many friends from it, she said "over time, however, success within the ranking has come to depend increasingly on visibility within the system itself," and that promotion of one's restaurant risked becoming as important as the work happening inside it.

She appears not to have been alone. Many of the world's top chefs consider awards and lists to be a tricky balance with their work at their establishment.

"Guides and lists now shape the industry they claim to assess," Ivan Brehm, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Nouri in Singapore, told CNBC. "Their judgments influence where diners spend money, where investment flows, which chefs get attention and what younger cooks learn to aspire to."

He added of the 50 that "none of this denies the brilliance of the restaurants on the list or the opportunities it has created for all of us. The question is what it sidelines and what it incentivizes."

Roš' Instagram post had more than 10,400 likes as of Friday, including from top chefs worldwide.

"The reaction and the reach were unexpected" from the post, Roš told CNBC in an email. "I did not write a post to provoke, but more as an eye-opener. And apparently a lot of people have the same perception as I do."

The 50 defended its list and role within the industry.

"The 50 does not claim to have the capabilities to police establishments on their budgets or surrounding activities," a spokesperson told CNBC. "The 50's role is to provide a snapshot of expert opinions on the best hospitality experiences in a given region or category. It does not act as a regulatory body and it applies the same voting rules and principles consistently across all countries and regions."

The time and effort put into the contest can be a dilemma, too.

"We didn't go to Lima this year. It's a huge amount of time in the air for a very short amount of time on the ground and, like Ana, I felt my time was better spent being present in our restaurant with our team and our guests," said Dave Pynt, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Burnt Ends in Singapore, who won the 50 Best Chef's Choice Award in 2017.

However, he added that "when the awards are closer and easier to get to, I love going. They're fun, you learn a lot, and you get to catch up with mates and people in the industry you don't get to see enough."

#2967. Norris claims pole in São Paulo, but Hamilton relegated to back

Lando Norris conquered thunderstorms to claim pole for the São Paulo Grand Prix while Lewis Hamilton suffered an engine nightmare and will be forced to start Sunday's race from the back of the grid.

In treacherous conditions at Interlagos, McLaren's Norris rose to the top as he pipped compatriot George Russell on the track – but the Mercedes man will start seventh after a five-place grid drop.

Kimi Antonelli, who deposed Norris as world champion last weekend, will be promoted to second while Liam Lawson – whose Racing Bulls seat remains in serious doubt – is a remarkable third on the grid.

Russell was again furious with his Mercedes team, questioning why they asked him to back off on his final flying lap – believing that decision cost him pole.

Ferrari announced ahead of final practice that both of their warring drivers would take 10-place grid drops, with Hamilton and Leclerc changing their internal combustion engine to incur the penalty.

But Hamilton immediately hit trouble. First he complained of vibrations from his new engine before having to come in again with his car pouring smoke from the back.

Ferrari subsequently decided that his entire power unit needed replacing, meaning the 41-year-old will need a miracle on Sunday.

Hamilton will be joined at the back of the pack by Max Verstappen, with the pair changing several engine components. Leclerc will line up 14th after his own penalty, leaving a scrambled order for what is expected to be a wet race.

Thunderstorms hit the track in the moments after final practice and it poured down again as qualifying began.

Some cars were in the pit lane when rain started and they tiptoed round before the session was swiftly red-flagged. Red Bull's Isack Hadjar was on the radio to ask for a new race suit, having been drenched.

"I'm so wet!" added the new world champion Kimi Antonelli. The 20-year-old remained in his car as the delay ticked past an hour.

Safety car driver Bernd Maylander did exploratory laps of the track, reporting back that it was "not driveable" as the weather worsened – with thunder bellowing over Interlagos.

Thoughts swiftly turned to this race in 2024, where qualifying was postponed until early Sunday morning.

"Definitely the wettest conditions I've experienced in a race car, in a car full stop I think!" Haas' Ollie Bearman said. "That's a lot for someone growing up in the UK."

Despite rain continuing to fall and the track still soaked, the FIA confirmed a restart for 4.26pm local time – an hour and 24 minutes after the red flag.

As the cars sat in the pits, the rain intensified again but the action resumed as Cadillac's Valtteri Bottas led the grid into the unknown on the lesser-used extreme wet tyre.

Home favourites Gabriel Bortoleto and Rafael Camara both banged the barriers – the latter remarkably continuing – but overall it was an extraordinary display by the drivers to keep their cars on track in appalling conditions.

Verstappen was third-fastest after Q1 but jumped out of his car to end his day early, knowing that he would start the race at the back regardless after a penalty for changing several engine components.

It is a position he is familiar with having done the same here over the last two years, famously winning from 17th in the rain in 2024.

Hamilton did not take the same approach as he began Q2 but when the grid switched to intermediates, the 41-year-old decided enough was enough and to save his tyres.

The second session came to an early halt when F1 debutant Camara drove into the barriers at Curva da Juncao, leaving McLaren's Oscar Piastri eliminated.

Russell was on the radio to say it was getting very dark on track as the day light disappeared.

Heavy rain fell again as the cars went out again but Norris showed his world champion quality, putting his car over a tenth clear of Russell.

The 28-year-old barked at his Mercedes engineer, believing he had been cost pole as his season of frustration continued.

"Congrats to Lando but that was a pole lap and a misjudgement on our side," Russell said.

#2966. Democrat shaping AI policy bought chip stocks despite backing congressional trading ban

Rep. Josh Gottheimer, a Democrat helping write his party's artificial intelligence agenda, has disclosed new purchases of AI-linked stocks — just months after voting to ban lawmakers and their families from buying individual shares.

The New Jersey Democrat disclosed an Oct. 12 purchase of between $1,001 and $15,000 of Benchmark Electronics stock, according to a filing released this week. His jointly owned Morgan Stanley account also bought up to $15,000 worth of Applied Materials shares on Oct. 26, following three earlier purchases of leading AI chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices.

While there's no evidence of wrongdoing, the trades put Gottheimer's investments in the spotlight as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress in January, gaining new power to regulate and investigate the AI industry.

Gottheimer, a former Microsoft executive, sits on the House Financial Services subcommittee overseeing AI in finance, has introduced legislation on AI regulations and co-chairs a commission developing Democrats' broader AI agenda.

Gottheimer's spokesman, Haraden Bottomley, said the congressman turned over control of his investments to a third party with "full investment discretion" before taking office in 2017.

"Gottheimer is also awaiting approval from Congress of a blind trust. In the meantime, he has taken the extra step of setting up an independent trust," Bottomley said.

Democrats are already targeting the AI industry. Reps. Maxine Waters and Greg Casar introduced a resolution opposing taxpayer bailouts for AI companies, referred in part to Gottheimer's committee. Casar has also called for AI executives to testify under oath, while other Democrats are pushing to restrict data centers or halt new construction altogether.

The purchases also contrast with Gottheimer's longstanding support for restricting congressional stock trading. In July, he was among just 13 Democrats who voted for the Stop Insider Trading Act, which would prohibit lawmakers and their families from purchasing most individual stocks. The bill passed the House but has not become law. Some Democrats opposed it both because it contained a voter-ID provision and because some said it should have gone further in curbing stock purchasing and holding by elected officials.

Four years earlier, Gottheimer backed legislation requiring lawmakers to place investments in blind trusts and pledged to establish one himself.

The AI purchases are just a small slice of Gottheimer's sprawling investment portfolio. The five trades totaled between $5,005 and $75,000, compared with $15.1 million to $59 million in disclosed household assets, including up to $44.5 million in stocks. His household has reported more than 1,100 securities transactions over three years, according to Follow the Filings. Gottheimer reports household assets jointly with his wife, Marla Tusk, who is general counsel at a political consulting firm.

Advanced Micro Devices competes with Nvidia in AI chips, while Applied Materials supplies semiconductor manufacturing equipment. It was also Benchmark's largest disclosed customer in 2025, accounting for 14% of revenue, according to a securities filing.

Benchmark, meanwhile, generated 30% of its third-quarter revenue from semiconductor equipment and another 16% from advanced computing and communications, according to its Oct. 28 earnings report. Gottheimer also disclosed an Applied Materials purchase in August and a sale in September.

The purchases came shortly before a sharp selloff in AI stocks. The Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.5% Thursday, as Nvidia dropped more than 9% and AMD roughly 17%. The index includes both companies.

Benchmark fell nearly 17% over Wednesday and Thursday, closing at $59.34 — roughly 25% below its closing price on Gottheimer's October purchase date — before recovering somewhat Friday.

#2965. How a third-quarter run amplified the Lakers’ resiliency

LOS ANGELES — Luka Dončić stepped up to the free throw line in the third quarter.

The Lakers' NBA Cup court plastered in gold. The crowd an ocean of the same color. Dončić was engulfed, but instead of the sound of waves flooding the senses, it was the MVP chants, each growing louder than the next.

Dončić made his first, marking his 40th point of the game and 20th in the quarter, capping off a 34-15 run in the frame that brought Los Angeles back from a 16-point deficit and gave them an 87-75 lead over the Portland Trail Blazers.

It was a run that put the Lakers in front heading into the final frame and helped them exact revenge on a Blazers side that handed them one of their two losses as they won 124-118 on Friday.

"Anytime you start a half down, you want to get to momentum early," head coach JJ Redick said of the third quarter. "We were playing from behind the first half, so I thought it was really important to get off to a good start, and we did that. … for the most part, it felt like we had control of the rest of the game, and a lot of that had to do with how we defended in the third quarter."

It's impossible to gloss over Dončić in this game, who finished with 53 points on 17-of-33 shooting from the field, passing Kareem Abdul-Jabbar for the 10th-most 50-point games in NBA history. The Lakers made their run and took control in the third quarter, but the momentum shifted when Dončić drained a shot from the opposite 3-point line as time expired in the first half.

"[That shot] changes the whole energy," Dončić said postgame. "In the team and in the gym. That was an important shot to make. So that's why I decided to make it."

At the time, the Lakers had made just three of their 23 3-point attempts and were enduring what looked like the game where the offense inevitably regressed to the mean. Instead, including Dončić's heave, Los Angeles made eight of their last 14 deep shots en route to a much-needed win.

Redick has preached patience and steady incline as a team all year, but a win like this for sole position at the top of the Western Conference — and on NBA Cup night no less — was a clear measure of their resilience.

And yet, Redick thinks they're still building.

"I think we're already a really together group," he added Friday. "You don't become a resilient team in 10 games, five games, 20 games. It takes a full season to build that resiliency. … But, the fact that this is a very together group and we've already shown a level of resiliency, it's exciting, it's promising and I want to continue building on that."

Much of this lands on Dončić's shoulders. He's having a historical season, one of the best we've ever seen, but he's a product of the team around him. It's a roster he had his hands all over, collectively crafted around his needs at every corner, a point driven by Portland head coach Micah Nori before they clashed on Friday.

Nori says it's Dončić's MVP to lose, "hands down," but because of everything around him. The shooting, the defending, Walker Kessler's rolling. It's all working in unison.

The Lakers have 72 games to go. It's not always going to be like this. But again, a night like Friday works wonders for the marathon Redick is trying to run.

"We all want good results," he said. "This is going to be a long process and it's going to take time for us to become a great basketball team. I like that our guys have responded to some adversity. Tonight was a great example, where we get down pretty big early and we just stick with it."

You can follow Connor on Twitter at @cmorenoNBA.

#2964. Data center double-down: Hassett says blue wave won't stop AI build-out

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett said Friday he expects data center growth to continue as projected, despite the blue-wave election that saw voters overwhelmingly favor limiting the tech infrastructure buildout.

Hassett, on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," acknowledged the dozens of measures on states' midterm ballots seeking to constrain data centers, a crucial part of President Donald Trump's push to win the global artificial intelligence race.

In at least 46 of the roughly 50 such initiatives on ballots in 2026, majorities voted to restrict development of the facilities, according to Ballotpedia's tally.

"We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

"And the stuff after that, you know, there's plenty of room in the U.S. for data center growth to continue, as it was projected ahead of the election," he said.

Hassett said he expects data centers will continue to expand in states with more welcoming governors, and he suggested that even hostile states will eventually come around.

"That's exactly what's going to happen," he said. "That, or what'll happen will be that the benefits of the data centers will be so obvious that people decide to change their minds about the way they regulate them."

Hassett also blamed Thursday's tech-driven market plunge on "uncertainty about what the political change is going to mean for data centers going forward."

"We've now got a ... new majority that maybe is going to go in there and muddle around in there, and I think that markets are anxious about that," he said.

"But I think that the economic strength that we see everywhere else is so strong, and of course, in the end, you could take something that's growing as fast as the data centers and make it grow a little bit slower, and you still have a blockbuster economy."

"And so, I think we're still very, very optimistic about what's going to happen going forward," he said.

The CNBC Voter Poll found 56% of midterm voters identified data centers as either a crucial or major issue — and of those who considered it crucial, 62% favored the generic Democratic candidate.

Trump has strongly embraced AI and data centers and has even tried to reverse their seeming unpopularity by rebranding the tech as "super intelligence," or SI.

In a Truth Social post Friday, Trump made clear that despite the midterm losses, his support for the nascent industry has not faltered.

"As President, I am 100% in favor of Super Intelligence (SI), and will work with the many States in the U.S. that want to become RICH by having them," Trump wrote. "They are GOLD, and only foolish leadership will say 'NO' to the riches they bring!"

#2963. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. #2962. NFL betting advice: Giants-Eagles picks and props

Welcome to the New York Giants betting angle, Week 9 edition! Each week, I'm giving you my three favorite wagers for that week's New York Giants game. My one rule: I won't ever give you wagers that go beyond -120. No gimmes!

The Giants are coming off their Bye week and heading into what has been a personal house of horrors. Big Blue hasn't won a game in Philadelphia since 2013, but for the first time since 2009, they're favored at the Linc. That tells you a lot about how this season has gone for Philadelphia, losers of six straight.

The first NY-PHI game of this season looks a lot different than it did when the schedule came out. The starting quarterbacks for this game aren't Jaxson Dart and Jalen Hurts. Oh, no. They're not even Jameis Winston and Tanner McKee, who opened the season as the respective backups. No, the starters in Week 9 for these NFC East rivals are J.J. McCarthy and Joe Milton III, each of whom wasn't even on his current team a few short weeks ago. McCarthy has played just five quarters on the Giants and Milton has yet to take a snap for the Eagles. He's also making the first start of his NFL career after appearing in five games across two seasons.

On top of that, there are injury concerns with both starting running backs. Cam Skattebo (hamstring) is listed as doubtful and Saquon Barkley continues to battle through hamstring and back soreness. He'll play, but how much?

What does this all mean? It means I'm flying a little blind this week with my bets. Be careful out there.

My picks were 1-2 in Week 7 and I'm 12-9 on the season. I'm looking to get back in the win column this week.

Let's get to this week's picks. All lines and odds are from Fanduel and are as of Saturday, November 7.

The line for the game is Giants -3, with a total of 38.5.

Game Total OVER 38.5 (-115). This game scares me and while I think the Giants will win, I don't want to lay the points or take the moneyline, given all the bad history of their visits to this building. I do like the over, even though Philly's offense has been atrocious and hasn't topped 13 points in three straight. Milton and more Barkley should give them a spark. Philly's defense has a lot of talent and might give McCarthy some problems, but New York has been scoring and should put up points. This is also a make-or-break game for the Eagles' chances to stay in the race in a weak NFC East, and I think they'll compete. Weather won't be a factor. This game offers the lowest total on the board this week — grab the over.

Devin Singletary OVER 55.5 rushing yards (-114). This is a volume play, and my favorite prop on the board for the entire slate. The Giants are going to want to run the ball, and with Tyrone Tracy, Jr. on IR and Cam Skattebo doubtful, Singletary should get a major workload. They don't have any other backs on the roster with a rushing attempt this season. The Eagles are allowing 131 rushing yards per game which is the fifth-most in the NFL. A lot of that is game script, but this is a favorable matchup regardless. Singletary is competent enough to hit this prop by the end of the third quarter at the latest.

Joe Milton III UNDER 189.5 passing yards (-114). Saquon Barkley played just 19 snaps each of the last two weeks while dealing with injuries. I'm guessing he'll get a bigger workload this week, to help take pressure off Milton in his first start. DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks are out for the third straight game, so Philadelphia is once again going to need to rely on tight end Dallas Goedert plus a bunch of unproven and inexperienced pass catchers. Milton has a big arm but struggles with accuracy, and the Giants' pass defense has been playing really well. They held C.J. Stroud to just 153 yards in a game that went to overtime in Week 7. Tyler Shough only threw for 144 yards against them the week before. Take the under.

Those are the picks for Week 9. Good luck with your wagers! And if you play fantasy football, be sure to check out my Week 9 fantasy preview, with start/sit advice and more, right here.

#2961. Egypt sacks coach Hossam Hassan over 'negative impact' on team image

CAIRO (AP) — Egypt coach Hossam Hassan was sacked on Saturday for what the soccer federation cited as "a negative impact on the positive image of the team."

The Egyptian Football Association did not elaborate in a statement, adding only that "maintaining the team's stability is a paramount priority" that cannot be compromised.

Egyptian media believed the decision had more to do with Hassan's personal life than rumors of disagreements with Egypt players including star forward Mohamed Salah.

Egypt's Supreme Council for Media Regulation banned Hassan last week from appearing in all media outlets, an unprecedented decision against a national coach. That followed disputes in Hassan's personal life becoming public.

Hassan was appointed Egypt coach in 2024 and became the first former Egypt player to coach the national team at a World Cup this year. Egypt was knocked out by Argentina in the last 16.

The EFA immediately replaced Hassan with Ali Maher, a long-time head coach in the Egyptian Premier League currently leading fourth-placed Ceramica Cleopatra.

Maher must get quickly up to speed. Egypt plays Malawi on Nov. 13 and 17 in qualifying for the 2027 Africa Cup of Nations. Egypt leads Group B.
