#2960. What makes this 76ers team stacked with superstars so unique

What makes this 76ers team stacked with superstars so unique originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

They're a team of superstars that don't play superstar basketball.

That's the beauty of this 76ers team. They play a brand of unselfish team-first basketball you rarely see from a team loaded with stars.

LeBron James is one of the two or three greatest players in NBA history, a four-time MVP, a four-time NBA champion, a 1st-ballot Hall of Famer.

Joel Embiid is a seven-time all-star, a former MVP, an Olympic gold medalist and a likely Hall of Famer.

Jaylen Brown is a five-time all-star, an NBA champion, a career 20 points-per-game scorer.

Tyrese Maxey is a two-time all-star, one of only two NBA guards averaging 27.0 points per game over the last four years.

Four certified megastars on one team. And somehow they've figured out how to leave their egos at the front door and play smart, tough, disciplined team basketball, the style of ball you just never see on these superstar-laden teams.

On offense, they patiently work the ball around, always looking for the open man or a positive matchup. You've got this bunch of big-name, high-priced All-Stars who are just as happy to pass the ball as shoot it. That never happens.

The 76ers are sixth in the NBA in assists, and they're one of only four teams with twice as many assists as turnovers. Smart, unselfish, patient, disciplined.

When an all-timer like LeBron averages 7.3 assists per game that speaks volumes. LeBron is fifth in the NBA in assists per game among forwards, which is wild. You can see the joy on his face when one of his passes leads to a basket. When one of the greatest players in history has that mentality, it rubs off on everyone around him.

Embiid hasn't really got going yet on offense and still takes too many 3's, but he's also embraced the team-first mentality, and his 4.8 assists per game are more than one full assist above his career average and 4th-most in the NBA among centers or forward-centers. And still one of the most dominating defensive players in the league.

Brown is averaging 27.0 points, which you expect, but he's also averaging 4.3 assists per game, significantly above his career average of 3.8 coming into the season. He's fit in so well so fast you forget its his first season here.

And what can you even say about Maxey? Just a beast. He's averaging nearly 30 points per game but never wastes a possession, always makes the right decision with the ball in his hands and is still finding time to average 4.3 assists per game.

With James, Embiid, Brown and Maxey, the 76ers are the only NBA team with four guys averaging at least 16 points and 4 assists per game. Only the Pacers, Magic and Raptors have three.

But where we're really seeing this group playing out of their minds is on the defensive end, where this bunch of all-stars, including two newcomers, has been astounding.

The 76ers have the 2nd-best defensive efficiency rating in the league, behind only the Pistons, who they play Wednesday night, and they're allowing the 2nd-fewest points in the league, also behind Detroit. Last year, they allowed 123 points per game through eight games. This year they're at 111.

They're holding teams to 43 percent shooting from the field, 3rd-best in the league and their lowest since 2011-12. Their 9.3 steals per game are 6th-most.

All five starters are among the top 25 players in the league in defensive rating – James is fourth, Embiid 11th, Edgecombe 15th, Maxey 22nd and Brown 23rd. How is that even possible?

It's rare to see superstar-caliber guys play as hard on the defensive end as the offensive end. Everybody on this team embraces it. They hustle, they work, they communicate and most importantly they commit to it.

The biggest concern when the 76ers built this dream team was that it would be a team of individuals out for themselves, but the reality has been the opposite.

In a very short period of time, they've learned how to play together. How to play for each other.

And we haven't even mentioned V.J. Edgecomb, who at 21 years old has figured out how to assimilate into this loaded lineup, never force anything, wait for his opportunities and make huge shots when he gets his turn. Edgecombe is at 12.7 points and 3.0 assists per game, and his numbers are understandably down a bit from last year, but if that bothers him it sure doesn't show.

And the role players – Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, Jabari Walker – have each adopted the same ego-free style of basketball, supporting the starters however they need to.

Nick Nurse deserves a ton of credit for getting this bunch to play this brand of hoops. It's not easy to get any NBA player to put team goals ahead of individual goals. But he's got Hall of Famers and all-stars doing it and having a blast along the way. They all seem to love playing basketball the right way and they seem to love playing together.

The 76ers are 7-1 and looking like a real threat to win the East, and they're doing it by playing the game the right way.

By committing to each other and not themselves.

#2959. Microsoft plans to use fewer neoclouds as AI buildout shows signs of strain

Microsoft is shrinking its roster of neocloud partners as it focuses on work with a few key suppliers and builds out its own data centers, according to people familiar with the matter.

The software giant's plans pose another potential challenge for a corner of the artificial intelligence market that's showing signs of strain due to rising borrowing costs, hefty debt loads and concerns about whether the buildout will slow down.

Microsoft has forged relationships in recent years with a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave, Nebius, Chronoscale, IREN, Lambda and Nscale, as well as with Oracle, which is pouring money into building out AI infrastructure.

Microsoft is now in the evaluation stage and hasn't decided which neoclouds it will stop using, said two sources, who asked not to be named because the talks are confidential. One of the people said Microsoft wants to prioritize its own data center footprint. Bloomberg reported on the plans earlier. Two people said that Microsoft opted not to renew a deal with Oracle.

A Microsoft spokesperson said in an emailed statement that its infrastructure strategy is "built on flexibility and choice."

"We combine Microsoft-owned datacenters, leased facilities and strategic partnerships with third-party providers to deliver the capacity our customers need, where and when they need it," the spokesperson said. "We regularly review these investments against customer demand. That includes tracking delivery and adjusting plans as circumstances change."

The neoclouds have been a turbulent trade on Wall Street in recent weeks as rising interest rates added to fears that many companies have become overly reliant on debt in rushing to build capacity. Shares of CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all down by roughly half over the past month even after rallying on Friday.

A recent industry review counted 323 neoclouds, and experts have predicted that many of them won't survive.

In its earnings report on Thursday, IREN said it handed over data center capacity to Microsoft in August, but Microsoft requested a networking equipment upgrade that will result in the infrastructure coming online in January or February instead of this year.

Microsoft finance chief Amy Hood said on the software company's fiscal first-quarter earnings call in October that some cloud contracts involving graphics processing units were delayed until the first half of 2027, affecting Azure cloud growth. Microsoft reported $45.2 billion worth of capital expenditures and finance leases during the latest quarter, and said capex spending will accelerate in the second half of the fiscal year.

CoreWeave and Nebius are both scheduled to report quarterly results next week. IREN shares popped 16% on Friday after the company said AI revenue more than doubled from the prior quarter to $200 million. IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level."

However, he acknowledged the challenge that market conditions present.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call.

Microsoft has already slashed its work with U.K.-based neocloud Nscale, walking away from a site that it had intended to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported this week that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. Nscale filed to go public in mid-September but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

Microsoft's big turn to neoclouds came after the launch of ChatGPT in 2022, which led to a surge in demand for GPUs. In looking elsewhere, Microsoft signed up CoreWeave, which was a privately held startup at the time with roots in cryptocurrency mining. When CoreWeave went public last year, it said 62% of its 2024 revenue came from Microsoft.

At the time of ChatGPT's release, OpenAI only used Azure. Now the company's cloud suppliers also include Amazon, Cerebras, CoreWeave, Google and Oracle.

Microsoft's spokesperson said that its approach to reviewing neocloud investments is "a normal and responsible way to manage large, complex infrastructure projects," and that "the pace of our new agreements reflects what we need today."

—CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2958. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2957. Maryland's 2nd half turnaround leads to 34-29 win at Purdue to snap long losing streaks

WEST LAFAYETTE, Ind. (AP) — Malik Washington ran for two scores and threw for two more Saturday as Maryland overcame a sluggish first half to get its first win in nearly two months, 34-29 at Purdue.

The Terrapins (3-6, 1-5 Big Ten) snapped a six-game losing streak and a 13-game skid in conference play. It gave Aazaar Abdul-Rahim his first win since being named interim coach following the firing of Michael Locksley.

Washington was 13 of 18 for 253 yards with one interception. He also rushed eight times for 32 yards despite wearing a brace over his left knee in the second half. DeJuan Williams had 14 carries for 99 yards and scored on a 75-yard run. Chris Durr Jr. finished with four catches, 127 yards and a nifty 87-yard TD catch that essentially sealed the win.

Purdue (3-6, 2-4) has lost three straight. Ryan Browne was 24 of 38 for 296 yards and lost a fumble. Fame Ijeboi and Xavier Townsend each had a scoring run. But the Boilermakers had four red-zone trips end with field goals.

Washington wasted no time showing everyone how different the second half would be.

He capped the first possession of the third quarter by hooking up with Na'eem Abdul-Rahim Gladding on a short screen pass that Gladding took 44 yards for a score to give Maryland a 14-12 lead. Washington converted on the next series with 1-yard scoring plunge after Browne fumbled at the Boilermakers 2-yard line.

Purdue answered with Townsend's 30-yard TD run as the teams were still settling down from two shoving matches and three personal foul calls. That made it 21-19.

Williams' long run on the next offensive play made it 28-19.

A bad snap on third-and-goal inside the 1-yard line resulted in a 3-yard loss and forced the Boilermakers to settle for another field goal.

Then Durr caught a screen pass on second-and-19, broke multiple tackles as he raced all the way across the field and outran the defense for a TD to give Maryland a 34-22 cushion.

Purdue cut the deficit to 34-29 on Ijeboi's TD run and drove to Maryland's 11-yard line with 5 seconds to play. But Browne was sacked on the game's final play.

Maryland: The Terrapins lethargic offense produced just five first downs and 108 total yards in the first half, but whatever Abdul-Rahim said at halftime worked. They looked like a different team in the second half, hitting big play after big play in what may have been their last chance this season to end their Big Ten skid.

Purdue: The Boilermakers certainly will regret this loss. They settled for four field goals in the red zone and couldn't get the Terrapins stopped in the second half. A win would have put Purdue at .500 in league play — a solid step forward for second-year coach Barry Odom. Instead, they'll be trying to snap another losing streak next weekend.

Maryland defensive back Dontay Joyner took another twist Saturday when he was ejected for a flagrant personal foul just 96 seconds into the game. He got into it with a Purdue player on the game's second play. Three plays later, he was called for a shoving a player at the end of a play.

Purdue kicker Jacobo-Echeverria Lozano shattered the school's single-season record for a freshman with his first of five field goals Saturday. He made kicks from 47, 26, 24 and 36 yards to give the Boilermakers a 12-7 halftime lead. His 22-yard field goal in the second half gave him 21 this season — five more than the previous record.

Maryland: Hosts the Badgers next Saturday.

Purdue: Travels to No. 21 Iowa next Saturday.

#2956. Raiders Mailbag: Should Las Vegas consider trading Tre Tucker?

The Week 9 matchup against the San Francisco 49ers marks the halfway point of the Las Vegas Raiders' 2026 season, and the NFL trade deadline is coming up on Tuesday. So, we have a lot to go over for this week's mailbag. Let's get to it!

A: It depends on how they feel about re-signing Tre Tucker in the offseason. If John Spytek and Co. don't want to prioritize Tucker, then it would make sense to get something for him, especially for a Top 100 pick. However, the Raiders are still in the hunt, so I don't think they're going to be looking to move on from starters at the deadline.

As far as re-signing Tucker goes, I don't think Jalen Nailor's contract impacts that much. Tucker has already out-produced Nailor, with almost double the amount of yards (2,040 to 1,179) and catches (152 to 81) and has more touchdowns (14 to 12) despite being in the NFL for one fewer season. Nailor is making less than $12 million per year (ranks 40th among WRs, per Over The Cap), and I'd bet Tucker gets something similar to Jakobi Meyer's contract with the Jaguars ($20 million per year, 27th among WRs).

Also, it's not ideal, but the Raiders can get out of Nailor's contract if that becomes an issue in the offseason. Per OTC, they'd save $5.5 million and take on about $9.4 million in dead cap with a post-June 1 cut, and it's about a 50/50 split with $7.4 million in savings via a pre-June 1 trade, and a little more than $12 million in savings with a post-June 1 trade. Again, not an ideal situation, but there are options available.

Personally, I feel like the need for a 'WR1' is kind of diminishing by the week. The offense and passing game are functioning pretty well with Brock Bowers as the top option, while the team is struggling to find answers at guard and defensive tackle. As long as Bowers is around, I wouldn't mind rolling with a collection of cheaper 'WR2s' like Tucker and allocating more resources to the trenches.

However, then the issue becomes: When and how much does Bowers want to get paid? He's going to be eligible for a contract extension in the offseason and will reset the tight end market whenever he gets his second contract. Plus, the offense's current 'WR1' will probably look to get 'WR1' money from the Raiders, which is in the $30-plus million per year range.

Q: Might there be any indication on how the Raiders will handle the trade deadline? Or do they have good poker faces?

A: Coincidentally, my power rankings roundup column had some trade deadline talk in it, where ESPN dove into the best in-season trade the Raiders have ever had (DB Mike Haynes in 1983). I was trying to think of the second-best in-season acquisition the franchise has made at the deadline, and had a hard time coming up with one. The reason I bring this up is that, historically, the Raiders don't make many moves at the deadline, at least not as a 'buyer'.

Considering that win or lose, they'll still be in the playoff hunt after this weekend, I have a hard time seeing Kirk Cousins getting traded. On top of that, the only significant trade rumor coming out of Las Vegas right now is that Maxx Crosby is not on the market. So, maybe a smaller deal like Powers-Johnson getting traded happens, but anything else would be a surprise at this point.

I get the feeling Spytek and Klint Kubiak are content to roll with the roster as is for the rest of the season and then address the needs in the offseason.

A: Again, I don't think we'll see any splash trades coming out of Vegas in the next few days. But to answer the question: Maxx Crosby, Kirk Cousins, Tucker (third-fourth fringe), Kolton Miller (third-fourth fringe), and Ashton Jeanty.

Crosby would have to be for a haul, and it sounds like the organization is trying to make it known that he isn't going anywhere. I touched on this in more detail last week; I'd hold onto Cousins and Miller to help support Fernando Mendoza. And I'll save y'all some time rather than rehash my Tucker and Jeanty takes. The running back will come up again later.

Q: Under Rob Leonard at defensive coordinator, Eric Stokes doesn't look as good as he did last year. At what point do they let all 3 young CBs play both CB spots? Did the league catch on to Rob Leonard's scheme, or why does the defense look so bad compared to the first 3 weeks?

A: The problem is that Darien Porter and Hezekiah Masses are both inconsistent right now, and Jermod McCoy's tape is pretty ugly on the limited snaps he's played this season. So, while Stokes has regressed, he's still probably the most reliable corner on the roster. As long as the Raiders are in the playoff mix, I don't think the coaching staff pivots to the full-on youth movement. But once they fall out of contention, then we could see Porter, Masses and McCoy get more opportunities.

As for Leonard's scheme, opposing offenses have definitely been picking on his tendacies in the last several weeks. The first two games had a lot of mystery about what he was going to run, making it difficult for the Dolphins and Chargers to put a typical game plan together. Also, we now know those are two of the worst teams in the league.

Even the Saints started to figure out Leonard, putting up 27 points in three quarters before turning the ball over on every possession in the fourth. New Orleans still racked up 381 yards, and that was our first sign that the book was out on the Raiders' defense.

The Chiefs were the first team to really highlight this; the Raiders can't stop the run consistently against 12 and 13 personnel. I dive into that in more detail when talking about the defensive tackles. I think part of it is a talent issue to cut Leonard some slack.

Ultimately, with the way it's currently constructed from scheme and personnel perspectives, this is a very turnover-reliant defense. That's hard to sustain because it requires the other team/offense to make mistakes.

Q: Why are the Raiders struggling to find a NT for their 3-4? Do they not have to correct players on the roster? Seems like they had numerous opportunities to grab one in the draft or through a trade. Yet they shuffle through players who don't seem to fit a true NT but are serviceable. Do they not hold NT an important position? Seems very neglectful by Spytek if your base is 3-4.

A: They didn't prioritize the position at all in the offseason. The starting nose tackle right now is Foley Fatukasi, who was signed after the first preseason game. As you mentioned, there were plenty of opportunities to address it in the offseason. This draft class had several good nose tackles, but Spytek went in a different direction every time.

I think that came down to two factors. One being that the front office wasn't going to be able to fill every need in one offseason. Think about how many starting spots were up for grabs or were questionable heading into training camp.

The other factor is that the 3-4 discussion was kind of a fallacy, where Rob Leonard ideally runs a variety of fronts. In the first few games of the season, the Raiders were in more even fronts out of nickel personnel. Then, teams started to realize the defense struggles to stop 12 and 13 personnel. Leonard likes to counter those looks by putting an off-ball linebacker (typically Quay Walker) on the line of scrimmage at edge to get into an odd front.

That's where not having a good nose tackle really started to show up because teams like the Chiefs, Bills and Rams just stayed in 12 and 13 personnel, forcing the Raiders to play fronts that they aren't adequately equipped to play.

So, I think it was a combination of negligence from Spytek and the defense not functioning the way Leonard envisioned in the offseason. Granted, you could argue that the former is the cause of the latter. It's certainly time for Spytek to place a bigger priority on the defensive tackle position as a whole than he has in the past.

Q: What's your take on Jonah Laulu getting waived & claimed by six teams versus the defensive tackle room, with Dont'e Thornton Jr. getting reactivated in his place and then inactive for the game?

A: I don't have a problem with Laulu getting waived. He was playing poorly, and defensive tackle was going to be a major offseason need even if he was still on the roster heading into the spring. But seeing six teams put in a claim for Laulu certainly feels like a missed opportunity to at least pick up a seventh-round pick instead of letting him go for nothing.

However, to play devil's advocate, just because a team is willing to claim a player for free doesn't necessarily mean they'd be willing to give up anything for him, even if it is a small price tag. Look at Darrell Luter Jr. The Raiders were one of five teams to put a claim in for him after cutdown day.

Maybe the 49ers didn't try or think they could get anything for Luter, but that's a highly functional organization that was in a similar situation to the Raiders with Laulu, and both clubs handled it the same way. Meanwhile, Luter has strictly been a special teamer in Las Vegas, and people would probably be pissed if they gave up a draft pick for that.

Leonard tried the 5-2-4 looks with Tommy Eichenberg serving as the base package linebacker against the Rams. Vegas was in base personnel 61.8% of that game, per Sumer Sports, which is about 40% higher than the rate for the entire season. But it didn't matter, as Los Angeles still ran the ball for 200 yards. However, Eichenberg had a very good game last week and was a big reason the defense got stops and was better against the run in the red zone.

So, we have conflicting information on the topic. But I wouldn't be surprised if Eichenberg gets another shot at having a bigger role this Sunday considering how well he played in New York. It's not like the Raiders are keeping points off the board in nickel!

Q: Will Fernando Mendoza start this year? Is it coaching and organizational malpractice to not play him?

A: Call me a pessimist, but I have a hard time seeing the Raiders winning more than one of their next four games (at 49ers, vs. Seahawks, at Broncos and at Browns). Granted, I think they can pull off an upset in Cleveland. However, at 5-7 heading into December, it'd be stretching the definition of "in the hunt" to consider Vegas a legitimate playoff contender at that point. So, I think there's a good chance Mendoza's first start comes after the bye in Week 14.

I won't go as far as saying it'd be "malpractice" not to play him this year, but I will say that they need to take the training wheels off at some point and let him get live reps. It might not be pretty, but let him work through the struggles now and have something to build on heading into 2027.

I understand why Klint Kubiak didn't do it, but I would have made the change ahead of last week's game. I get wanting to be patient and have been on board with that strategy from the jump. However, now that we're halfway through the season, making a quarterback change needs to be a bigger discussion within the organization, and sitting Mendoza for the entire campaign wouldn't be doing him any favors, in my opinion.

The Raiders still have a chance at making the playoffs right now, and Cousins is playing well. So there's no need to make a change right now. But once the postseason becomes an afterthought, it's time to turn it over to the rookie.

A: I think so. It's only been one game, but Trey Zuhn III has already played better than Caleb Rogers did in any of his starts this year, for example. Also, Masses is going back and forth with Porter despite being drafted two rounds later, and Mike Washington Jr. is pushing Ashton Jeanty for the lead running back role despite being drafted three rounds later.

The 2026 draft class may not be perfect, but it's already surpassing last year's, and the top pick has barely seen the field!

A: I think Jeanty's situation last week was a combination of both. The running game hasn't been working (that's the earth-shattering analysis you're here for, right?), and while it wasn't/isn't all his fault, Jeanty wasn't making it better.

Kubiak had to try something else and see what Washington could do as the feature back. However, I think the dramatic 25-2 split was to send a message to Jeanty and was partially punitive for his stupid 'bulletin board' comments about the Jets' defense.

Moving forward, I think it will be closer to a 50-50 workload between the two backs. The rushing attack was better at staying on schedule with Washington last week, but his lack of vision also led to some missed opportunities for big runs, and the Jets were down their best run defender, defensive tackle T'Vondre Sweat. In other words, the rookie had a decent performance to increase his workload from what it was earlier in the season, but he didn't run away with the job.

As for what Jeanty's future looks like in Las Vegas, that's a good question that I don't have a great answer for right now. Sunday could help give us more clarity. But if I'm calling the shots and another team was willing to give me, say, a second-round pick for Jeanty on Tuesday, I'd have a hard time turning that down. I don't think that will happen; however, depending on how the next couple of months play out, it could be a big topic in the offseason.

Q: Should Jackson Powers-Johnson be playing center instead of guard, or does he not have the skills to play in the Raiders system(s), or do the abilities that made him successful in college not translate to the NFL?

A: I've always felt like JPJ's best position is at center, skill-wise at least. I think the problem is that he wasn't able to get the mental part of the position down, which led to communication issues in the trenches. In other words, he was executing his assignment but wasn't getting the rest of the line on the same page, and the latter is half the battle for a center. It'd be like having a quarterback who has a strong arm but can't read defenses, anticipate throws, etc.

That's why I think the Raiders tried Powers-Johnson at guard, to let his physical tools flourish and take the thinking off his plate. But, for whatever reason, it hasn't worked out. Some guys can make that transition naturally; others just never feel comfortable not being at the pivot.

The other problem is once Vegas gave Tyler Linderbaum $81 million in the offseason, that ended any ideas of JPJ playing center moving forward. Linderbaum hasn't lived up to the contract, but he's been better than JPJ has and has the superior resume of the two. That's why I've been saying since his benching that Jackson Powers-Johnson's days in Vegas are likely numbered.

A: I feel like that's pretty spot on. Based on what we've seen so far, I think this year's Raiders are a frisky, seven- to eight-win club. Not quite a playoff team, but floating around an average ranking. Seeing as I thought they'd only win five games heading into the season, that's a good starting point for a rebuild.

That'll do it for this week's mailbag. #2955. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2954. Why a wave of bomb threats is proving so difficult for Europe's aviation authorities to contain

A spate of bomb threats against passenger flights has forced diversions, emergency landings and military escorts across European airspace this week, stoking fear among passengers and heaping pressure on airlines.

Authorities have described the more than two dozen threats as malicious hoaxes, and no actual attacks have been carried out. But the incidents have highlighted the huge impact that even false claims can have on a tightly scheduled aviation network.

"This is really quite exceptional, I've never seen this before in my whole career," Wilfried Covent, head of security firm Covent Consulting with a specialization in aviation, told CNBC by phone.

"We've had bomb threats in flights and towards airports, but usually maybe a single copycat, one or two max. What we see now is a systematic attack on airlines and airports."

An email sent to European broadcasters on Friday claimed to be from the leader of the group behind the plane hoaxes and also said bombs had been placed in their buildings, prompting evacuations. No bombs were found at the sites.

"What happened this week is just a small part of what awaits Europe in the near future," the message said, according to Reuters.

The news agency said it could not verify the sender's identity, where the emails originated or whether they were linked to the threats against flights.

Covent said the "criminals" — who many security analysts say are likely to be state-backed actors working in coordination to disrupt public life for Europeans, but have not been identified — were well aware that the "weakest point of aviation is targeting it in-flight."

"With a threat on an airport or a general threat to aircraft, you can assess the situation without fully disrupting operations. When the threat is called on a specific flight number that is in the air, even with a hoax, that triggers international regulations which ensure you don't ignore it — it means a quick landing, evacuation and searches by specialist forces," he told CNBC.

"For the context of an assessment, you cannot say that 20 times before it has been a hoax so this time it is a hoax, the safety of passengers and crew is priority number one. The criminals know that."

Responsibility for dealing with hoax attacks is split, international agencies told CNBC. Assessing security threats and implementing aviation security measures primarily falls to national authorities, working in coordination with airport operators, airlines and law enforcement.

Air traffic control will also be involved in finding aircraft with an appropriate place to land, while the European Union Aviation Safety Agency, or EASA and U.K.'s Civil Aviation Authority more broadly set aviation safety rules and set how airlines prepare their crews to respond to security threats. Within the European Union, the European Commission is involved in aviation security policy and coordination between member states.

The operational decisions also depend on where the threat arises. Joji Waites, director of flight safety, policy and regulation at the British Air Line Pilots Association, said airline assessors grade a threat as red, amber or green, but that an amber verdict would never be given if the plane is airborne.

If assessors judge a threat credible, the crew is informed and the captain takes command of decisions for the aircraft, guided by company procedures. On the ground, that may mean an evacuation, while in the air the priority is to land as soon and as safely as possible.

As well as the fear and disruption to specific flights, each diversion can then affect the next flight scheduled to use that aircraft, the crew's working hours and passengers with connections.

The network effect can be significant. Philip Baum, a visiting professor of aviation security at Coventry University, told AP that a single diversion could cost an airline as much as $250,000, including replacement crews, missed connections and disruption to the rest of the day's schedule.

"Diversions necessitated by these bomb threats are a disturbance to a system designed for predictability and efficiency, and lead to increased workload for pilots, Air Traffic Controllers and airports," an EASA spokesperson said in emailed comments.

"As these staff have all have been trained to cope with such unexpected developments, the immediate safety threat from individual hoax threats is low. However, the higher number of diversions places an additional strain on the system."

National governments will already be looking at their aviation authority regulations and frameworks and there will be discussions at the European and global level, John Edwards, senior consultant at Gates Aviation in London, said by phone.

"Bomb threats to aviation have been going on for decades, what they'll need to be looking at is what's different. Why have there been so many, why have so many been considered sufficiently credible to warrant the kind of action that it's warranted?"

"There's a range of questions they should be able to work through fairly quickly to establish a broad-brush level of credibility. If there is some characteristic that is new, every time there is an event of this kind, it triggers a big collaboration to ensure recurrence is prevented going forward."

"In theory, he said, "the vast majority should be able to assessed and considered non-credible based on routine risk assessment processes and well established procedures."

However, Eric Schouten, a former intelligence liaison officer at the Dutch General Intelligence and Security Service and CEO of security consultancy Dyami, said that changes to existing procedural regulations would take some time and require balancing the views and responsibilities of countries, airports, safety authorities and the European Union, with geopolitics also playing a role.

"There is a high likelihood even more flights are going to be canceled or diverted. It's possible that next to be targeted could be railways and highways," he said.

"But they will figure it out. It's not the first time airlines have had to deal with hoax calls because there are always copycats."

"Whatever measures they come out with to address it, they won't share publicly" for risk of alerting the bad actors, he added.

Wilfried Covent said that while there would not be any quick changes to assessment protocol, "if this is really going on for ever and ever something will need to change or you'd have to stop flying completely."

"Based on what we've seen in the past with other kinds of threats, time goes on and the type of attack may change and move on," he continued, pointing to the wave of cyberattacks that has recently hit supermarket fridges and blood banks.

#2953. Maresca's dream start turns into nightmare as title hopes hit

Enzo Maresca left the City Ground on Saturday evening with chants of "can we play you every week?" ringing in his ears - and the Italian's dream start as Manchester City manager is rapidly turning into a nightmare.

Amid a sea of noise from the home faithful on the banks of the River Trent, the delirious Forest supporters witnessed their side produce a thrilling performance as City sunk to a third straight Premier League loss.

Maresca's leaky backline continues to make defensive blunders and once Josko Gvardiol gave the ball away for Jair Cunha to open the scoring in the sixth minute, City were on the back foot.

With Arsenal edging past Hull earlier in the day and keeping another clean sheet, City head into the international break searching for answers after a 3-1 loss and six points behind the champions.

Maresca said: "The first half we didn't match [Forest's] intensity or win duels or second balls. The first thing required in these games is intensity.

"We tried to react at 2-0 but it's too late. We scored one, probably could have scored more but in transition we concede one more. No panic. We need to understand but no panic."

Asked why the intensity was low, Maresca replied: "I need to analyse that."

The situation already looks bleak for City, but what does history show?

Should Brighton fail to beat Brentford on Sunday, Arsenal would be just the fourth side in Premier League history to have a six-point lead after 10 games, while only two teams have been so far behind as City at this stage and gone on to win the title.

One of those was Manchester United in 2002-03. The other? City themselves in 2013-14 under Manuel Pellegrini.

But Fabian Hurzeler's Brighton have the opportunity to leap up to second place and five points behind the Gunners should they beat Brentford.

There is another factor which looms large over City's campaign.

The club have lodged an appeal after being found guilty of breaking Premier League financial rules.

That process is ongoing - and it is not yet known what potential sanctions City could face.

But - even if any possible punishment does not affect this season - their title hopes are looking slimmer by the match.

Delightful Delap and outfought Anderson - Forest v Man City player ratings

Has Anderson lived up to the hype after Man City move?

With his hands tucked inside his trouser pockets and his head bowed, a forlorn-looking Maresca paced the touchline towards the end of the match.

The City boss said during the week he was still getting to know his players, but performances like the one against Forest will provide some clarity as to who he can, and cannot, trust.

After starting the season with seven straight wins, City have hit real trouble with defeats against Aston Villa, Brighton and now a pumped-up Forest.

Their problems at the back are not helping as it was another game where individual errors cost them dearly - and former striker Liam Delap took full advantage of the lapses in concentration with two goals.

Gvardiol was at fault for the first, Iliman Ndiaye and Elliot Anderson got in a muddle for the second, while Matheus Nunes did not put a challenge in for the third.

"Before the game all the talk was about individual errors," former Forest winger Steve Stone told BBC Radio 5 Live. "Apart from the first goal, this hasn't been about that - the line is disjointed.

"The full-backs were very wide. If the ball turns over quickly and you pass it with your first pass you can be right through them. They have been exposed today.

"Oliver Glasner's team have played through them every time."

It was a miserable return to the City Ground for Anderson since his £116m move to City in the summer and the midfield dynamic alongside Enzo Fernandez does not seem to be working.

The Argentine should have scored with a headed chance from close range but spent much of the game complaining in referee Sam Barrott's ear - City team-mates and supporters may be expecting more from their club record signing.

The former Chelsea player scored in the midweek Champions League win at RB Leipzig, but that is his only goal or assist in his past nine games.

Maresca needs to work out how to get the best out of Fernandez during the international break.

Stone added: "I came here tonight thinking City would win, I thought they had chances. This is a real hammer blow to them, there is something not right about the back four at the minute.

"I have never seen a City team give the ball away so much in the middle of the park."

Rayan Cherki's stunning strike gave City hope but they could not turn it around and the numbers following the result make for grim:

City have lost three consecutive Premier League games for the first time since a run of four in November-December 2024

They have become the second team in English top-flight history - after Sheffield Wednesday in 1904-05 - to win their first seven games of a season and then lose their next three

This was their first league defeat by two or more goals against a side starting the day in the relegation zone since November 2008 (a 2-0 loss to Bolton Wanderers).

Maresca was forced to chop and change his defence again, withdrawing both Gvardiol and Marc Guehi at half-time because of injuries, according to the manager.

It meant Nunes came in at right-back, with Abdukodir Khusanov moving inside, and Nico O'Reilly playing down the left.

Former Liverpool midfielder Danny Murphy told BBC Sport: "I thought City's performance was poor in terms of mentality.

"To make so make so many mistakes when you go anywhere in the Premier League, but especially to a team who are fighting for the lives, and you know they're going to come after you.

"They were sloppy with the ball, not winning duels. I would even probably go as far as saying there was probably a bit of arrogance at the start of the game from them.

"I think that there are now some big questions to ask the manager about how he gets them back on track. I think with the talent they have got, it is not going to be a big problem.

"But I think defensively, maybe sticking to the same backline for a little while is the way forward."

#2952. SB Nation Reacts Results: Ch-Ch-Changes

We've got our SB Nation Reacts results for this week ready to roll, so let's take a look at what fans of the Minnesota Vikings have to say about our favorite team this week, shall we?

Our first question for this week, as it always is, has to do with whether or not everyone is confident with the direction of the team. And, coming after the first loss of the season in Week 8, the answer is a resounding, "No. . .no, we are not."

Of those who responded to our poll for this week, just 17% are confident that a team currently sporting a 6-1 record is moving in the right direction. On the surface, that sounds a bit absurd, but honestly. . .it's not terribly difficult to understand. The offense, once again, struggled to score against a defense that it really shouldn't have struggled against, and the defense eventually just wore down as the offense wasn't able to stay on the field. It's the same formula that we've seen repeatedly this season, and something has to change going forward.

Which leads us to our second question for a week, which asked about whether or not the team needed to make a change at quarterback. Now, keep in mind that we posted this question before the team formally made the announcement that Carson Wentz would start at quarterback against the Buffalo Bills on Monday, but I'm not sure how much it would have affected the results.

Of those who responded to our poll, 78% agreed that a change needed to be made at quarterback in some form. There were 45% who said that the Vikings should go to Wentz, which is what has happened. . .so far. Another 33% believe that the Vikings should make a trade, which is something that could still happen before the NFL trade deadline, which arrives this Tuesday at 3:00 PM Central Time. The other 22% believe that the Vikings should stay the course at quarterback, which simply tells me that there were more Bears, Lions, and Packers fans voting in the poll than usual this week.

As a bit of a bonus question this week, GA Skol has put a poll over in the feed about whether or not the shift to Wentz gives the Vikings a better chance to win on Monday night against the Bills.

Those are our SB Nation Reacts results for this week, ladies and gentlemen! Thank you, as always, to everyone who took the time to participate in this week's polls, and keep your eyes out for next week's questions!

#2951. Sixers-Cavs by the numbers: Pressure, threes and a 29-point rematch

Sixers-Cavs by the numbers: Pressure, threes and a 29-point rematch originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers and Cavaliers have already played one game that looked like two.

Cleveland led by 29 points during the Sixers' home opener on Oct. 22 and carried a 69-45 advantage into halftime. Philadelphia responded by winning the second half, 69-37, and completing a 114-106 comeback.

It was the Sixers' largest comeback since the NBA began maintaining detailed play-by-play data in 1997-98. Only nine other teams during that span had overcome a deficit of at least 29 points.

Fifteen days later, the teams will meet again Friday night at Rocket Arena. This time, the matchup counts toward the NBA Cup standings.

The 8-1 Sixers have won five consecutive games and opened group play with a 98-93 victory over the Knicks. The 4-4 Cavs have dropped two straight and will make their Cup debut.

Here are five numbers that help frame the rematch.

Philadelphia's comeback required more than a hot shooting stretch.

The Sixers committed 15 turnovers in the first half, allowing Cleveland to build a 22-6 advantage in points off turnovers. Their pressure then changed the game after halftime.

The Cavs committed 21 turnovers by the end of the night. What had been a 16-point Cleveland advantage in points off turnovers finished even at 22 apiece.

"They upped their pressure and physicality," Cavs head coach Kenny Atkinson said after the game. "It was pretty clear. We did not handle it well."

The Sixers scored 64 of their 69 second-half points through their starting lineup. Tyrese Maxey finished with 28 points, and Philadelphia turned a game that appeared finished into a franchise-record comeback.

The first half of the opening meeting now looks like an exception.

The Sixers have committed 12.9 turnovers per 100 possessions this season, the NBA's second-lowest rate. They are also third in turnover differential, averaging 3.4 fewer giveaways than their opponents.

That is notable for a team that entered the season intending to play faster. Philadelphia has pushed the ball without routinely surrendering possessions.

LeBron James' presence as the primary facilitator has helped. So has Maxey's willingness to move the ball when defenses trap him. Joel Embiid has averaged 5.2 assists, third among qualified centers, and the Sixers have increasingly trusted VJ Edgecombe to make decisions late in games.

Cleveland's defense has not forced many mistakes. The Cavs enter Friday near the bottom of the league in opponent turnover rate.

The first meeting swung when the Sixers stopped giving away possessions and forced Cleveland into mistakes of its own.

The Sixers have allowed 105.1 points per 100 possessions, the NBA's second-best defensive rating. Cleveland enters with the league's ninth-ranked offense.

The Cavs are the only top-10 offense the Sixers have faced through their first nine games.

Philadelphia has held opponents to a 50 percent effective field-goal percentage and 31.8 percent shooting from three-point range. Its defensive shot profile has been encouraging, too. Only 46 percent of opposing three-point attempts have been classified as wide open, the fifth-lowest share in the league, and opponents have taken just 22 percent of their threes from the corners.

There is reason for caution. Based on the quality of shots Philadelphia has allowed, only Washington's opponents have underperformed their expected effective field-goal percentage by a wider margin. The Sixers have defended well, but opponents are unlikely to remain this cold indefinitely.

Cleveland, with Donovan Mitchell, James Harden and Evan Mobley, can test whether those numbers hold against high-level individual shot creation.

The Cavs have attempted exactly 42 three-pointers per game and made 37.2 percent of them.

Cleveland went 7 for 29 from beyond the arc in Philadelphia. Mitchell, Harden and De'Andre Hunter combined to shoot 3 for 17. The Cavs scored 60 points in the paint and still reached 106, but their 24.1 percent performance from three prevented them from stopping the Sixers' comeback.

Friday's game could look considerably different if Cleveland generates its normal volume.

The Sixers have controlled the three-point line well so far, but Mitchell is coming off a 27-point performance against Oklahoma City and scored 32 in the first meeting with Philadelphia. Harden added 16 points that night, although his six turnovers became costly as the Sixers increased their pressure.

Philadelphia has been outrebounded by 5.1 per game. The Cavs rank sixth with 47.5 rebounds per contest.

The Sixers have already shown they can survive a major deficit on the glass. They beat Milwaukee by 11 despite getting outrebounded by 22. That is not a formula they will want to test regularly against Cleveland's size.

Mobley had 18 points and eight rebounds in the first meeting. Jarrett Allen added 14 points and seven boards, helping Cleveland win the rebounding battle, 46-44.

Whether Allen plays Friday remains uncertain.

Cleveland listed Allen as questionable because of a right knee injury after he left Wednesday's loss to Oklahoma City in the first quarter. Allen collided knee-to-knee with Thunder forward Chet Holmgren and did not return.

The Cavs also listed Peyton Watson as questionable with right hamstring soreness and Tyrese Proctor as questionable with an ankle injury. Proctor left Wednesday's game during the fourth quarter. Jaylon Tyson is out with a right ankle sprain.

Allen's availability could significantly alter the matchup on the glass. If he sits, Thomas Bryant would likely handle more of the center minutes behind Mobley. If he plays, Cleveland will have the same starting frontcourt that outrebounded the Sixers during the first meeting.

Atkinson has emphasized the possession battle during Cleveland's two-game slide.

"You've got to get more shots than the other team," he said this week. "Rebounding is a big part of it."

That also carries added importance in the NBA Cup, where point differential is the second tiebreaker after head-to-head results.

Philadelphia enters Friday at 1-0 with a plus-five differential in East Group B. Miami is also 1-0 at plus-20, while Cleveland is playing its first group game.

The Sixers already know they can erase 29 points against the Cavs. A cleaner start would be far more useful than proving it twice.

#2950. Rams Add Dual-Threat RB, Two-Time Pro Bowler Amid Myles Garrett News

The Los Angeles Rams will officially have Myles Garrett on the field against the Washington Commanders in Week 9.

This will be Garrett's first game since Week 1 against the San Francisco 49ers in Australia. LA announced the news that they officially activated Garrett off injured reserve, along with several other roster transactions, on the eve of game day.

Activated from Reserve OLB Myles Garrett, RB Ronnie Rivers

Activated, from Practice Squad (Standard Elevation) LS Jake McQuaide, CB Damarion Williams

That is something McVay also suggested was possible if not likely earlier in the week. McVay noted that the two-time Pro Bowler, McQuaide, would get the nod.

The Rams signed Williams to their practice squad in October. He figures to slot in behind Emmanuel Forbes, Josh Wallace, and Cam Lampkin in some capacity. It is unclear what capacity, though, since Trent McDuffie is also questionable after not practicing this week.

In that sense, Williams' elevation is an ominous sign for the Rams amid Garrett's return.

#2949. America's newest small business owners with no employees are off to a hot start in AI era

It takes hundreds of billions of dollars for the tech giants to scale artificial intelligence, but it takes just a few AI tools for the smallest companies in the U.S. economy to get up and running with a cost-competitive business model.

Alex Willen expects the 12 e-commerce brands he operates with no employees to generate between $2.5 million and $3 million this year. Kristin Ginn left Microsoft in March and started trnsfrmAItn, an AI-adoption consultancy. Seven months later, she has roughly $250,000 in business received or contracted through year-end with no employees or regular contractors.

"The biggest misconception is that it's just about individual productivity; it's actually about leveraging AI to perform tasks that previously required a whole team," said Stanford economist Erik Brynjolfsson.

Early research on the impact of AI within the solopreneur business world bears watching.

According to Liya Palagashvili, a labor economist and director of the Labor Policy Project at George Mason University's Mercatus Center, new business formation is rising in direct correlation to applications unlikely to become employer firms.

In industries highly exposed to AI, business applications without early signs of hiring rose 26.8% from the first quarter of 2024 through the first quarter of 2026, from roughly 222,000 to 281,000 per quarter.

Over the same period, applications in those industries with stronger indications of eventually becoming employer businesses fell 6.4%. In construction and wholesale trade, which Palagashvili used as a comparison group, non-employer-type applications declined 0.4%.

"What AI is doing is giving those capabilities directly into the hands of the individual," she said.

Palagashvili cautioned that the findings are preliminary and descriptive and do not establish that AI caused the increase. The Census-based measure identifies applications without indicators associated with near-term payroll or hiring plans; it does not show that those businesses ultimately remained one-person firms or never hired employees.

But Willen shows what the economics can look like.

Anthropic's Claude helps with bookkeeping, customer service, advertising, and inventory across his businesses, while OpenAI image tools perform work that once went to photographers and designers.

Without AI, Willen said, "I'd probably need one other full-time person, plus I'd need to use contractors for photography, design, bookkeeping, and ad management."

A traditional product photography shoot could cost $2,000 to $5,000, and a designer $300 to $1,000 for new listing images, he said.

When AI directly eliminates one of those expenses, Willen said, "that money just goes into my pocket."

AI has also allowed him to pursue revenue channels he would not have previously considered. After acquiring Lone Trail, a hunting-gear brand selling binocular harnesses, packs, and other outdoor accessories, Willen said its Shopify site accounted for roughly 1% of sales. Rather than shut down the channel, he used ChatGPT and Claude to rebuild the site and AI to help run Meta advertising. In a recent 30-day period, he said, Shopify accounted for roughly 30% of Lone Trail's revenue.

"That's going to end up being at least six figures of annual revenue that would not exist without AI," Willen said.

Ginn's business shows how quickly a new entrant can reach meaningful revenue without first building an organization around it. She uses AI to help with proposals, pricing, strategy, and other work and is exploring an AI agent to perform part of a labor-intensive client process.

A survey conducted by accounting and invoicing software company FreshBooks identified similar behavior more broadly. Seventy-nine percent of solopreneurs said they had tried AI before hiring someone for a task they could not perform themselves (the same figure was 86% among when including microbusiness owners). AI allows them to "look much bigger than they are," FreshBooks CEO Shaheen Javadizadeh said.

But among existing small businesses, the available data tells another story. Gusto, a payroll and HR software company that serves small businesses, tracked businesses after they adopted AI and found their headcount was about 7% higher after a year than a comparable businesses that knew about AI but had not adopted it. Among firms with fewer than 10 employees, AI adopters grew headcount about 10% more than comparable non-adopters.

Willen's experience also highlights a tradeoff in the new business model: operating with fewer people does not eliminate reliance on larger technology platforms. Roughly 80% of its overall revenue still comes through Amazon, and he uses Meta and Shopify to expand direct sales. Ginn has no staff, but LinkedIn has been one of the biggest drivers of customers to her consultancy, she said.

Brynjolfsson said the proliferation of smaller producers can actually increase the value of the platforms that connect them with the buyers.

"There's this long tail of products, but at the same time, big platforms like Amazon are more powerful than ever because they aggregate all of these and they bring them together," Brynjolfsson said.

But the relationship is not as simple as labor savings flowing directly to Big Tech. Willen said some of the money he saves becomes profit, while Ginn spends less than $60 a month on paid AI tools.

AI can make it economical for more small producers to exist. And as core business production becomes cheaper, small businesses place greater focus on customer acquisition and distribution, Brynjolfsson said.

Brynjolfsson believes that while solopreneurs will thrive, use of AI will at the same time lead to the creation of even larger firms, on average, than exist today.

In a September research report titled "AI's Use of Knowledge in Society," Brynjolfsson and Zoë Hitzig, a researcher at the Anthropic Institute who studies the social and economic impacts of AI, argue that as AI codifies knowledge previously scattered among workers and enables more information to be aggregated and acted upon centrally, firms will grow larger on average, while industry concentration increases and local managerial autonomy declines. The researchers say early evidence is largely consistent with those patterns.

That's bad news for medium-sized companies. Brynjolfsson predicts AI could contribute to a "hollowing out" of midsize firms — very small teams capable of doing more at one end and giant organizations exploiting AI and scale at the other.

"The fat in the middle — the layers of management focused on internal coordination — will likely be significantly reduced," he said.

Adam Branch has experienced a version of that shift from inside two very different technology companies.

Before founding rhino.ai, an enterprise software company that uses AI to modernize legacy systems, Branch built Incentive Technology Group, a traditional technology services company that grew to roughly 500 employees. Under that model, winning more work meant hiring more people, he said.

"With rhino.ai, we're building AI software to do more of the work those teams used to do," said Branch.

Rhino.ai has roughly 50 employees. Branch estimates comparable modernization work can now be performed with three to four times the productivity per employee.

His team estimates labor represented roughly 85% of operating spending at ITG, and technology about 5%. At rhino.ai, Branch puts labor at roughly 40%, while technology, including cloud, software, and AI models, represents about 25%.

AWS sees a similar loosening of the relationship between revenue and headcount among AI-native startups. Its Engines of Growth research, an independent global study of 3,413 startup founders and senior leaders across 20 countries, found those companies grew revenue an average 156% annually versus 65% for startups overall, while more than half generated over $400,000 in revenue per employee. The report focused on companies under five years old that use AI in its most advanced forms.

"Growth used to be tied directly to how many people you could hire," said Ruba Borno, vice president of AWS Specialists and Partners. "Now founders can scale with technology, not just headcount."

Brynjolfsson does not see the result as simply Big Tech winning at everyone else's expense. Entrepreneurs can keep some of the productivity gains, consumers get more choice, and platforms benefit from supplying the technology and connecting more producers with customers, he said.

"The platforms and the people who are creating the tools are winning because they are able to sell them, the solopreneurs are winning because they are able to use them, and the consumers are winning because they are getting new products that they didn't get before," he said. "It's mostly a bigger pie, so it's a win-win-win — a triple win."

What AI may change is the shape of that bigger pie, he said.

"Whenever there's a technology change, a platform change, obviously it benefits the companies that are creating those platforms and technologies," Brynjolfsson said. "But it also benefits new entrants who can start fresh."

#2947. Ankeny football coach recovering after scary sideline collapse

A scary moment occurred during the Iowa high school football state quarterfinal between Ankeny and Iowa City Liberty on Nov. 6.

Late in the fourth quarter, Ankeny defensive ends coach Greg Schoon collapsed on the Hawks' sideline. Several people in attendance, including police, athletic trainers from both teams and even fans who work in the medical field, came down to the field to assist with CPR and the use of a defibrillator until an ambulance arrived to take Schoon to a hospital.

After a delay, the game continued and the Hawks advanced to the state semifinals with a 51-29 win over Liberty.

Ankeny head coach Jeff Bauer told the Des Moines Register on Nov. 7 that he visited Schoon in the hospital following the game. Bauer said Schoon was "talking like normal coach Schoon" as the hospital ran tests. It was unclear what caused Schoon to collapse and he will remain in the hospital at least until Monday, Bauer said.

Bauer said the quick response from all who assisted was "amazing."

"We all said later, 'God, it's a blessing it happened there,'" Bauer said. "There's so many people there to help save you and they all went to action in no time."

Bauer said Ankeny players planned to visit Schoon in the hospital over the weekend. Ankeny will face Johnston on Nov. 13 at the UNI-Dome for a spot in the state finals as the program hopes for a speedy recovery for their beloved coach.

Eli McKown covers high school sports and wrestling for the Des Moines Register. Contact him at Emckown@gannett.com. Follow him on Twitter at @EMcKown23.

#2945. Many workers lose unused FSA money — but could still come out ahead. Here's how

Health flexible spending accounts, or FSAs, are tax-advantaged accounts that let workers set aside pretax money to cover eligible health expenses. However, they're often use it or lose it, with unspent balances forfeited at the end of the plan year.

About 4 in 10 workers with health FSAs forfeited unused money in 2024, according to an October report from the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonprofit research organization. Among those FSA account holders, the average forfeiture was $404, down from $436 in 2023. The research examined more than 3.3 million accounts representing $3.9 billion in contributions.

Yet even workers who don't zero out their balance may still benefit from using an FSA, compared with what they would have spent using after-tax dollars to cover the same expenses.

"Even if someone forfeits FSA funds back to their employer, they can still come out ahead — relative to not participating in an FSA — if the amount they forfeited is less than the tax savings they realized," said Jake Spiegel, a senior research associate for EBRI and author of the study.

Nearly three-quarters — 72% — of state and local government workers and 47% of private-sector workers have access to a health FSA in 2026, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That compares with 68% and 40%, respectively, in 2017.

FSAs differ from health savings accounts, or HSAs, which some people use in conjunction with high-deductible health plans. Although both let you set aside pretax money for health care expenses, HSAs let you roll over your balance from year to year, and even invest it.

Roughly a third of FSA users in the EBRI database face a strict use-it-or-lose-it provision, Spiegel said. The rest either are permitted by their employers to carry over an amount — up to $680 in 2026 — into the following year or are offered a grace period of up to 2.5 months after the deadline to incur additional expenses or face forfeiture. However, employers cannot offer both options.

While many plans have a Dec. 31 deadline for spending FSA money, some employers have deadlines falling in other parts of the year, said Rachel Rouleau, chief compliance officer for Health-E Commerce, parent company of online retailers FSA Store and HSA Store.

"Understanding these plan-specific deadlines is essential, so employees should check with their employer or FSA administrator to avoid forfeiting unused funds," Rouleau said.

Additionally, many employers give you extra time — typically three months after the deadline — to submit receipts for qualifying expenses that occurred during the previous plan year.

For 2026, the maximum contribution allowed to health FSAs is $3,400. In 2027, that amount will be $3,500, the IRS announced Thursday.

Part of the reason workers have forfeitures may be that they must decide how much they want to contribute to their FSA before the plan year begins.

"It's difficult to project how much you'll need to spend on health care, especially if you are particularly healthy and don't have a chronic condition with predictable prescription drug costs," Spiegel said.

Contributions to FSAs are withheld from paychecks and generally avoid federal taxes, as well as so-called payroll taxes — known as FICA — that go toward Social Security and Medicare. They also may avoid state and local taxes, depending on where you live.

To determine how much you're saving in taxes by using an FSA, you can add up those tax rates and apply the total to your contribution, said certified financial planner Sean Lovison, founder of Purpose Built Financial Services in Moorestown, New Jersey.

For example, say that your federal, FICA, state and local taxes total 35% and you contributed the maximum allowed — $3,400 for 2026. In this case, the calculation would result in $1,190 in estimated tax savings, which is "how much you're saving on taxes by contributing to your FSA," Lovison said.

The illustration assumes the worker receives no employer contribution to the FSA and the tax rate applies to the entire contribution.

"As long as the number you're forfeiting from your FSA is less than that number, you're going to come out ahead," Lovison said.

Of course, he said, "you don't want to walk away from FSA money, but at the same time, from a mathematical standpoint, you're still coming out ahead."

If you face a forfeiture, you can use it to help inform your decision on how much to put toward your FSA for 2027, if you haven't already made benefit elections for next year.

"Employees should look at their anticipated healthcare expenses over the coming year and consider both their daily health routines and expected annual expenses," Rouleau said. "This includes recurring prescriptions, doctor visits, copays, deductibles, dental and vision care, and other eligible out-of-pocket healthcare expenses for themselves and their eligible dependents."

Before you resign yourself to a forfeiture, keep in mind that there are also many ways to use the money on eligible expenses before the end of the year.

"There are thousands of ways consumers can responsibly spend down their FSA dollars, from everyday healthcare essentials to eligible telehealth services," Rouleau said.

Additionally, "many consumers are surprised to learn that a wide range of over-the-counter medications, menstrual care products, sunscreen, first-aid supplies and certain health-monitoring devices are FSA-eligible," Rouleau said.

That's in addition to doctor and dentist appointments, prescription drugs and other healthcare services such as acupuncture and addiction treatment, according to the IRS.

If you're unsure whether you have money remaining in your FSA or need to spend it by the end of the year, most plans offer an online portal where you can review your balance, submit claims and check deadlines. The FSA administrator's information is typically listed on the back of your FSA card, Rouleau said.
